user
A research task, not a packaging task. Do not ask for design approval, do not run any
brainstorming or approval workflow. Proceed directly to work.

TARGET. The referee for this paper stated the threshold explicitly:

  "仅修正文献和补充技术细节可使定理成为可信、可发表的结果,但不足以显著改变 JFA 层级判断。
   要达到该层级,最有效的增加是任意阶的 law-by-law 非负尾缺陷分解,或覆盖 Gaussian、稳定
   及更一般核与散度的抽象机制定理。"

That is: fixing citations and filling technical gaps makes the theorems credible and
publishable but does NOT change the tier judgement. What would is either

  (a) an ARBITRARY-ORDER law-by-law nonnegative tail-defect decomposition, or
  (b) an abstract mechanism theorem covering Gaussian, stable and more general kernels
      and divergences.

Pursue (a). It is the nearer target because the order-two case is already ours.

CURRENT STATE, in the referee's own description:

  "稿件已经在协方差阶给出有限二阶矩下的 law-by-law 分解,将通用二次项与未展开远尾的
   非负 Φ-能量分离,并以该能量的消失作为精确充要条件。"

So at covariance order, under a finite second moment, the manuscript already separates the
universal quadratic term from a nonnegative Phi-energy of the unexpanded far tail, and
proves that the vanishing of that energy is an exact necessary and sufficient condition.
Read the paper and locate that decomposition precisely before doing anything else. Note
that the moment theorem itself now covers all orders r >= 1 after the recent extension;
it is the DECOMPOSITION that is still confined to order two.

WHAT TO DO.

 1. Determine whether the order-two decomposition generalises to arbitrary order r: a
    law-by-law splitting of the order-r defect into a universal term plus a NONNEGATIVE
    tail-defect energy, with vanishing of that energy an exact iff characterisation, under
    the appropriate finite-moment hypothesis. Nonnegativity and the exact iff are the two
    features that give the order-two result its force; a generalisation that loses either
    is not the target.
 2. If it generalises, prove it in full and state it as a theorem with complete hypotheses.
    Identify precisely which step of the order-two proof carries over and which needs a new
    argument, and say so in the text.
 3. If it does NOT generalise, find the exact obstruction and state it. A sharp,
    well-identified obstruction at order r >= 3 is itself a real result and an honest
    outcome: report which step fails, why, and whether a weaker conclusion survives (for
    instance nonnegativity without the iff, or the iff under a stronger moment condition).
    Do NOT manufacture a weaker statement dressed up as the target, and do NOT claim
    partial success you cannot prove.

RULES.
 - This is the opposite of mechanically appending propositions. Do not add unrelated
   corollaries, do not pad the theorem count. One theorem, or one clearly stated
   obstruction, is the whole deliverable.
 - Every new claim must be proved at referee standard. If a step rests on numerics, say so
   explicitly - numerical checks are a sanity check and never substitute for a proof.
 - Do not weaken, restate or renumber any existing theorem.
 - Keep every .tex under 800 lines; split if needed.
 - Clean rebuild with .aux removed: latexmk -pdfxe exit 0, zero undefined references, zero
   undefined citations, zero multiply-defined labels.
 - Re-run every artifacts/verify*.py and artifacts/test_*.py; report pass counts. If you
   add a verification script, it must be deterministic: no wall-clock timings in any
   archived output, and two consecutive runs must produce byte-identical files.
 - No revision marks anywhere.

REPORT, honestly and specifically: whether the arbitrary-order decomposition holds; the
exact statement you proved or the exact obstruction you found; which steps of the
order-two argument survived and which did not; and the compile and test results. If the
answer is that it does not generalise, say that plainly in the first line of your report.

## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** â€” lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];

    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];

    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];

    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];
    "Has checklist?" -> "Follow skill exactly" [label="no"];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" -> "Follow skill exactly";
}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOPâ€”you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept â‰  using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" â†’ brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" â†’ debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

name: verification-before-completion
description: Use when about to claim work is complete, fixed, or passing, before committing or creating PRs - requires running verification commands and confirming output before making any success claims; evidence before assertions always
---

# Verification Before Completion

## Overview

Claiming work is complete without verification is dishonesty, not efficiency.

**Core principle:** Evidence before claims, always.

**Violating the letter of this rule is violating the spirit of this rule.**

## The Iron Law

```
NO COMPLETION CLAIMS WITHOUT FRESH VERIFICATION EVIDENCE
```

If you haven't run the verification command in this message, you cannot claim it passes.

## The Gate Function

```
BEFORE claiming any status or expressing satisfaction:

1. IDENTIFY: What command proves this claim?
2. RUN: Execute the FULL command (fresh, complete)
3. READ: Full output, check exit code, count failures
4. VERIFY: Does output confirm the claim?
   - If NO: State actual status with evidence
   - If YES: State claim WITH evidence
5. ONLY THEN: Make the claim

Skip any step = lying, not verifying
```

## Common Failures

| Claim | Requires | Not Sufficient |
|-------|----------|----------------|
| Tests pass | Test command output: 0 failures | Previous run, "should pass" |
| Linter clean | Linter output: 0 errors | Partial check, extrapolation |
| Build succeeds | Build command: exit 0 | Linter passing, logs look good |
| Bug fixed | Test original symptom: passes | Code changed, assumed fixed |
| Regression test works | Red-green cycle verified | Test passes once |
| Agent completed | VCS diff shows changes | Agent reports "success" |
| Requirements met | Line-by-line checklist | Tests passing |

## Red Flags - STOP

- Using "should", "probably", "seems to"
- Expressing satisfaction before verification ("Great!", "Perfect!", "Done!", etc.)
- About to commit/push/PR without verification
- Trusting agent success reports
- Relying on partial verification
- Thinking "just this once"
- Tired and wanting work over
- **ANY wording implying success without having run verification**

## Rationalization Prevention

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Should work now" | RUN the verification |
| "I'm confident" | Confidence â‰  evidence |
| "Just this once" | No exceptions |
| "Linter passed" | Linter â‰  compiler |
| "Agent said success" | Verify independently |
| "I'm tired" | Exhaustion â‰  excuse |
| "Partial check is enough" | Partial proves nothing |
| "Different words so rule doesn't apply" | Spirit over letter |

## Key Patterns

**Tests:**
```
âœ… [Run test command] [See: 34/34 pass] "All tests pass"
âŒ "Should pass now" / "Looks correct"
```

**Regression tests (TDD Red-Green):**
```
âœ… Write â†’ Run (pass) â†’ Revert fix â†’ Run (MUST FAIL) â†’ Restore â†’ Run (pass)
âŒ "I've written a regression test" (without red-green verification)
```

**Build:**
```
âœ… [Run build] [See: exit 0] "Build passes"
âŒ "Linter passed" (linter doesn't check compilation)
```

**Requirements:**
```
âœ… Re-read plan â†’ Create checklist â†’ Verify each â†’ Report gaps or completion
âŒ "Tests pass, phase complete"
```

**Agent delegation:**
```
âœ… Agent reports success â†’ Check VCS diff â†’ Verify changes â†’ Report actual state
âŒ Trust agent report
```

## Why This Matters

From 24 failure memories:
- your human partner said "I don't believe you" - trust broken
- Undefined functions shipped - would crash
- Missing requirements shipped - incomplete features
- Time wasted on false completion â†’ redirect â†’ rework
- Violates: "Honesty is a core value. If you lie, you'll be replaced."

## When To Apply

**ALWAYS before:**
- ANY variation of success/completion claims
- ANY expression of satisfaction
- ANY positive statement about work state
- Committing, PR creation, task completion
- Moving to next task
- Delegating to agents

**Rule applies to:**
- Exact phrases
- Paraphrases and synonyms
- Implications of success
- ANY communication suggesting completion/correctness

## The Bottom Line

**No shortcuts for verification.**

Run the command. Read the output. THEN claim the result.

This is non-negotiable.

\title[Cayley--Poisson and Stable-Kernel Entropy]{Cayley--Poisson Entropy and
Optimal Stable-Kernel Moment Exponents}
\author{Anonymous author}
\date{}

\keywords{Cayley transform, Poisson semigroup, isotropic stable kernel,
relative entropy asymptotics, optimal moment exponent, Cayley--Chebyshev
modes, nonlocal Bregman dissipation, regular variation}
\subjclass[2020]{47D07, 60E10, 94A17, 42A38}

% Compile root: main.tex.
% Clean bundle directory: submission_source/ contains only these active article inputs.

%   sec_appendix_conditional_tails.tex

\begin{document}
\def\JFAArticleMain{1}
\begin{abstract}
Chen and Niles-Weed proved exact first unmatched moment relative-entropy
asymptotics under Gaussian smoothing, in every dimension and at every order,
assuming sub-Gaussian tails
\cite{ChenNilesWeed2022SmoothedWasserstein}.  We extend this mechanism to
the heavy-tailed isotropic strictly \(\alpha\)-stable kernels,
\(0<\alpha<2\), which exclude the Gaussian endpoint, and replace their
strong tail hypothesis by a far weaker, optimal complete-moment condition.
If two
laws on \(\RR^d\) match all tensor moments below the integer order \(r\ge1\),
have a nonzero order-\(r\) difference \(\Delta_r\), and have finite
\(p_{r,\alpha,d}\)th absolute moments, where
\[
 p_{r,\alpha,d}=\max\left\{r,
 \frac{2r(d+\alpha)}{d+\alpha+2r}\right\},
\]
then
\[
 D_{\rm KL}(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\|p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\eta)
 =\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d,r}(\Delta_r)s^{-2r}+o(s^{-2r}).
\]
The coefficient is an orthogonally invariant positive-definite quadratic
form on symmetric order-\(r\) tensors.  The endpoint moment is sufficient.
When \(d+\alpha>2r\), every exponent
\(\rho\in[r,p_{r,\alpha,d})\) fails uniformly even for pairs with finite
nonzero \(\Delta_r\); when \(d+\alpha\le2r\), the threshold equals \(r\), and
smaller exponents fail only to define \(\Delta_r\) throughout the moment
class.  Thus the contribution is the heavy-tailed critical exponent,
endpoint sufficiency, and uniform sharpness, not the Gaussian quadratic
mechanism itself.

At covariance order we also give a law-by-law decomposition under finite
covariance alone: the universal quadratic term plus a nonnegative nonlinear
energy of the unexpanded tail beyond the smoothing scale.  For the Cauchy
kernel, the Cayley transform additionally yields the exact equivalence between
finite variance and bounded \(t^4\) relative entropy, an all-order finite
Laurent rule for the subsequent coefficients, and a fully justified
nonlocal Bregman tail identity for the moving matched quotient.
\end{abstract}

% sec_preliminaries.tex -- Preliminaries
\section{Cayley--Haar preliminaries}%
\label{sec:preliminaries}

We fix the notation and normalizations used throughout the paper.  The
same section also records the only circle-theoretic input needed later:
the Cayley chart identifies normalized Haar measure on~$\TT$ with the
Cauchy weight on~$\RR$.

Write
\[
\TT:=\{z\in\CC:\abs{z}=1\},\qquad
\DD:=\{z\in\CC:\abs{z}<1\},\qquad
\UU:=\Bigl(-\tfrac12,\tfrac12\Bigr).
\]
The normalized Haar measure on~$\TT$ is
\[
\dd m_{\TT}(e^{\ii\theta})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\dd\theta,
\qquad
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi].
\]
We write
\[
\overline\RR:=\RR\cup\{\infty\}
\]
for the one-point compactification of~$\RR$.  For $f\in L^{1}(\RR)$ we
use the Fourier transform
\[
\widehat f(\xi):=\int_{\RR}f(x)e^{-\ii x\xi}\dd x,
\qquad \xi\in\RR,
\]
so that $\widehat{f*g}=\widehat f\,\widehat g$ whenever both sides are
defined.
For $f\in L^{1}(\RR)\cap L^{2}(\RR)$ we also use the inversion and
Plancherel formulas
\[
f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR}\widehat f(\xi)e^{\ii x\xi}\dd\xi,
\qquad
\|f\|_{L^2(\RR)}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\|\widehat f\|_{L^2(\RR)}^{2}
\]
\cite[Chapter~I]{Stein1971}.

If $X$ is a real-valued random variable with density $f_X$ with respect
to Lebesgue measure, its differential entropy is
\[
h_{\mathrm{diff}}(X):=-\int_{\RR}f_X(x)\log f_X(x)\dd x,
\]
whenever the integral is well defined.  If $f$ and $g$ are probability
density functions on~$\RR$, the Kullback--Leibler divergence
\cite{KullbackLeibler1951,CoverThomas2006} is used throughout as an
extended nonnegative functional.  Precisely, if \(f\not\ll g\), then
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g):=+\infty .
\]
If \(f\ll g\), write \(u=f/g\) on \(\{g>0\}\) and set \(u=1\) on
\(\{g=0\}\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:extended-kl-definition}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)
:=\int_{\RR}\{u(x)\log u(x)-u(x)+1\}\,g(x)\dd x
\in[0,\infty],
\end{equation}
with the convention \(0\log0=0\).  Equivalently, when
\(f\log(f/g)\) is integrable in the ordinary sense, this equals
\[
\int_{\RR}f(x)\log\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\dd x .
\]
Thus the symbol \(D_{\mathrm{KL}}\) never presupposes finiteness.  In the
applications below \(g\) is a positive Poisson density, so absolute
continuity is automatic; under finite centred second moment
Lemma~\ref{lem:kl-bounded-test-and-poisson-quotient} gives
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}))<\infty\) for every
\(t>0\).  Without that finite-variance hypothesis, all KL statements are
read in the extended interval \([0,\infty]\).

\begin{lemma}[KL bounded-test and Poisson quotient estimates]%
\label{lem:kl-bounded-test-and-poisson-quotient}
\label{lem:bounded-test-pinsker}
\label{lem:finite-time-poisson-kl-finite}
\label{lem:sharp-threshold-bounded-test-pinsker}
The following elementary estimates will be used repeatedly.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Let \(f\) and \(g\) be probability densities on~\(\RR\), and interpret
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)\) by \eqref{eq:extended-kl-definition}.  If
\(\varphi\in L^\infty(\RR)\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prelim-bounded-test-pinsker}
\left|\int_{\RR}\varphi(x)\{f(x)-g(x)\}\dd x\right|^2
\le 2\|\varphi\|_\infty^2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g).
\end{equation}
The inequality is an extended-valued statement: if the right-hand side is
\(+\infty\), it is read as a trivial bound.

\item Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on~\(\RR\) with mean
\(\bar{\gamma}\) and finite centred second moment.  Define
\[
P_t(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t}{t^2+x^2},\qquad
h_t=P_t*\nu,\qquad g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad u_t=h_t/g_t .
\]
For every compact interval \(0<a<b<\infty\), the quotient \(u_t\) is bounded
above and below by positive constants uniformly for
\((t,x)\in[a,b]\times\RR\).  More precisely, with
\(\mu_2=\int(\gamma-\bar\gamma)^2\dd\nu(\gamma)\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prelim-poisson-quotient-two-sided-bound}
\frac{1}{2(1+\mu_2/a^2)}
\le u_t(x)\le 2+\frac{2\mu_2}{a^2}
\qquad(t\in[a,b],\ x\in\RR).
\end{equation}

\item Under the hypotheses of the preceding item,
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)<\infty\qquad(t>0).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the bounded-test estimate, if \(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)=+\infty\) there is
nothing to prove.  Otherwise \(f\ll g\), and the usual
Csisz{\'a}r--Pinsker inequality for natural logarithms gives
\[
\|f-g\|_{L^1(\RR)}^2\le 2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g).
\]
Since \(\varphi\) is bounded,
\[
\left|\int_{\RR}\varphi(f-g)\dd x\right|
\le \|\varphi\|_\infty\|f-g\|_{L^1(\RR)} ,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:prelim-bounded-test-pinsker}.

Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on~\(\RR\) with mean \(\bar{\gamma}\) and
finite centred second moment.  Define
\[
P_t(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t}{t^2+x^2},\qquad
h_t=P_t*\nu,\qquad g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad u_t=h_t/g_t .
\]
Write \(X_c=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\), \(z=(x-\bar{\gamma})/t\), and
\[
R_\varepsilon(z):=\frac{1+z^2}{1+(z-\varepsilon)^2}.
\]
Then
\[
u_t(x)=\int_{\RR}R_{X_c/t}(z)\,\dd\nu(\gamma).
\]
The elementary inequalities
\[
R_\varepsilon(z)\le 2(1+\varepsilon^2),
\qquad
R_\varepsilon(z)\ge \frac{1}{2(1+\varepsilon^2)}
\]
give, with \(\mu_2=\EE X_c^2\),
\[
\frac{1}{2(1+\mu_2/a^2)}
\le u_t(x)\le 2+\frac{2\mu_2}{a^2}
\qquad (t\in[a,b],\ x\in\RR),
\]
where the lower bound uses Jensen's inequality for
\(s\mapsto(1+s)^{-1}\).  Fix \(t>0\) and choose \(a<t<b\).  If
\(m\le u_t\le M\), then
\[
0\le D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\int_{\RR}u_t(x)\log u_t(x)\,g_t(x)\dd x
\le M\max\{|\log m|,|\log M|\}<\infty .
\]
\end{proof}
We shall repeatedly use the elementary identity
\[
\int_{\RR}(f-g)\dd x=0
\]
whenever $f$ and $g$ are probability densities.

% sec_covariance_proxy_defect.tex -- exact covariance-proxy KL defect
\section{The Covariance-Matched Poisson Defect}
\label{sec:covariance-proxy-defect}

Finite covariance determines the quadratic mode but, in dimensions at least
four, does not force the matched entropy to attain the corresponding
coefficient.  The following result identifies the complete nonnegative
remainder without imposing a tail class or an absolute moment above order
two.

\begin{theorem}[Covariance-proxy defect decomposition]
\label{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
\label{thm:finite-covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with mean
\(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
 h_t=P_t^{(d)}*\nu,
 \qquad g_t=P_t^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma),
 \qquad H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t),
\]
and let \(b_\Sigma\) be the bounded covariance mode in
\eqref{eq:multidim-leading-theorem-density-mode}.  For all sufficiently
large \(t\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy}
 k_{\Sigma,t}(x)
 :=g_t(x)\left[1+t^{-2}b_\Sigma
 \left(\frac{x-\bar\gamma}{t}\right)\right]
\end{equation}
is a probability density.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 :=t^4D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t}).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
 t^4H_d(t)
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+\mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)+o(1)
 \qquad(t\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No density of \(\nu\), regular-variation hypothesis, angular balance, or
higher absolute moment is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
After translation, assume \(\bar\gamma=0\), and write
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^{(d+1)/2}.
\]
In normalized coordinates,
\[
 q_t(y):=\frac{h_t(ty)}{g_t(ty)}
 =\EE F_y(X/t),
 \qquad \delta_t=q_t-1,
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma.
\]
The covariance calculation in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} gives
\(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\).  Since \(b_\Sigma\) is bounded,
\(1+u_t\ge1/2\) for all sufficiently large \(t\), proving that
\eqref{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy} is positive and has integral one.

We first establish the only asymptotic input needed below:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}
 t^2\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)\delta_t(y)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 \longrightarrow
 \int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)^2\,\Omega_d(\dd y).
\end{equation}
Set
\[
 J(z)=\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)F_y(z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y.
\]
The function \(b_\Sigma\) is even, smooth, and has bounded second
derivatives.  Translation in the last integral shows that \(J\) is even,
\(J(0)=0\), and its Hessian is bounded on the unit ball.  Hence
\(|J(z)|\le C|z|^2\) for \(|z|\le1\).  For \(|z|\ge1\), positivity and
normalization of the translated Poisson density give
\(|J(z)|\le\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty\le
\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty|z|^2\).  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing}
 |J(z)|\le C_{d,\Sigma}|z|^2,
 \qquad z\in\RR^d.
\end{equation}

Let \(B_2(y,z)\) denote the homogeneous degree-two Taylor term of
\(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Taylor expansion under the integral gives
\[
 J(z)=\int b_\Sigma(y)B_2(y,z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)+o(|z|^2).
\]
The covariance-mode identity proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} is
\(\EE B_2(y,X)=b_\Sigma(y)\).  Since \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\),
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing} and dominated
convergence imply
\[
 \EE[t^2J(X/t)]
 \longrightarrow
 \int b_\Sigma(y)\EE B_2(y,X)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d.
\]
Because \(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\), the left side is precisely the
left side of \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}.

We also have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness}
 \|\delta_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, Minkowski's integral inequality bounds this norm by
\[
 \EE\left\|P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-X/t)-P_1^{(d)}
 \right\|_{L^1(\RR^d)},
\]
which tends to zero by translation continuity and dominated convergence.

Put \(r_t=\delta_t-u_t\).  Equations
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing}
 t^2\int b_\Sigma r_t\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \|r_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Uniformly in \(y\),
\[
 \log(1+u_t)=u_t+O(t^{-4}).
\]
It follows from \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder}
 \int r_t\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d=o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
On the other hand, with
\(\Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s\), boundedness of \(b_\Sigma\) gives
\begin{align}
 \int(1+u_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 &=\int\Phi(u_t)\dd\Omega_d \notag\\
 &=\frac{t^{-4}}2\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d+O(t^{-6})
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+O(t^{-6}).
 \label{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy}
\end{align}
Combining \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy} yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic}
 \int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}

Finally, the exact logarithmic identity is
\[
 H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t})
 +\int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d.
\]
 All terms are finite for large \(t\).  Indeed, convexity of relative entropy
 and
 \[
  D_{\rm KL}\!\left(P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-z)\,\middle\|\,
  P_1^{(d)}\right)
  \le \frac{d+1}{2}\log\{2(1+|z|^2)\}
  \le C_d(1+|z|^2)
 \]
 bound \(H_d(t)\) by an integrable average under
 \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\).  The first inequality follows from
 \(1+|w+z|^2\le2(1+|w|^2)(1+|z|^2)\).  Moreover,
 \(\log(1+u_t)\) is bounded.  Substitution of
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic} proves
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}.  Nonnegativity of relative
entropy then gives \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Raw-tail Poisson-energy decomposition]
\label{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
mean \(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Write
\(\nu_c\) for its centred law, put \(a=(d+1)/2\), and define
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^a,
 \qquad
 \Phi(v)=(1+v)\log(1+v)-v.
\]
For \(t>0\), let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential}
 V_t^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>t\}}F_y(x/t)\,\nu_c(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}
 H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, the covariance-proxy defect in
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 =t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(1),
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No regular-variation or absolute-continuity hypothesis is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Translation permits us to assume that \(\nu\) is centred.  Set
\[
 \delta_t(y)=\int\{F_y(x/t)-1\}\nu(\dd x),
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma,
 \qquad \tau_t=\nu(|x|>t).
\]
Finite second moment gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness}
 \tau_t\le t^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Let \(\ell_y(z)\) and \(B_2(y,z)\) be the homogeneous degree-one and
degree-two Taylor terms of \(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Direct differentiation of
the rational function \(F_y\), together with translation continuity of
the first two derivatives of \(P_1^{(d)}\) in \(L^1(\RR^d)\), gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_\infty
 +\|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}
 \le \varepsilon_d(|z|)|z|^2
\end{equation}
for \(|z|\le1\), where \(\varepsilon_d\) is bounded on \([0,1]\) and
\(\varepsilon_d(r)\to0\) as \(r\downarrow0\).  The Taylor coefficients
themselves satisfy, for \(j=1,2\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms}
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_\infty+
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\le C_d|z|^j,
 \qquad B_1=\ell.
\end{equation}

Centering and the covariance-mode identity
\(\int B_2(y,x)\nu(\dd x)=b_\Sigma(y)\) now yield the exact split
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-quotient-split}
 \delta_t=u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t+R_t,
\end{equation}
where
\[
 R_t=
 \int_{|x|\le t}\{F_\cdot(x/t)-1-\ell_\cdot(x/t)
                   -B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x)
 -\int_{|x|>t}\{\ell_\cdot(x/t)+B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x).
\]
Equations \eqref{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus} and
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms} imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-remainder-smallness}
 \|R_t\|_\infty+\|R_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Indeed, after multiplication by \(t^2\), the interior remainder is
dominated by \(C_d|x|^2\) and converges pointwise to zero.  The omitted
linear and quadratic tail terms are bounded respectively by
\[
 Ct^{-1}\int_{|x|>t}|x|\nu(\dd x)
 \le Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x).
\]

The identity
\(F_y(z)\Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-potential-normalization}
 V_t^\nu\ge0,
 \qquad \int V_t^\nu\dd\Omega_d=\tau_t.
\end{equation}
We next justify the nonlinear separation.  If \(|u|\le1/4\) and \(v\ge0\),
two integrations of \(\Phi''(s)=(1+s)^{-1}\) over the rectangle joining
\((0,0)\) to \((u,v)\) give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-phi-interaction}
 |\Phi(u+v)-\Phi(u)-\Phi(v)|\le C|u|v.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness} and
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remainder-smallness}, all negative arguments in
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-quotient-split} are uniformly larger than \(-1/2\) for
large \(t\).  The mean-value theorem, the bound
\(\log(1+v)\le v\), and \eqref{eq:raw-tail-potential-normalization} then give
\begin{align}
 \int|\Phi(\delta_t)-
 \Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}),
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-remainder}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t)-
 \Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}),
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-mass}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu)-\Phi(u_t)-
 \Phi(V_t^\nu)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}).
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-interaction}
\end{align}
For clarity, the first left side is bounded by
\(C\|R_t\|_\infty\tau_t+C t^{-2}\|R_t\|_1
 +C\|R_t\|_\infty\|R_t\|_1\); the second is bounded by
\(C\tau_t(\tau_t+t^{-2})\); and the third is at most
\(C\|u_t\|_\infty\tau_t\).  These bounds are all \(o(t^{-4})\).

Finally, boundedness and zero mean of \(b_\Sigma\) imply
\[
 \int\Phi(u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 =\frac{t^{-4}}2\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d+O(t^{-6})
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}+O(t^{-6}).
\]
Since \(H_d(t)=\int\Phi(\delta_t)\dd\Omega_d\), equations
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remove-remainder}--
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remove-interaction} prove
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}.  Comparing it with
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition} proves
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition} and the equivalence.
\end{proof}

For a formulation involving only masses of moving sets, define
\[
 A_{t,0}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ |x-ty|<t\},
\]
and, for \(k\ge1\),
\[
 A_{t,k}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ 2^{k-1}t\le|x-ty|<2^kt\}.
\]
Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-functional}
 M_t^\nu(y)=(1+|y|^2)^a\left\{
 \nu_c(A_{t,0}(y))+
 \sum_{k\ge1}2^{-k(d+1)}\nu_c(A_{t,k}(y))\right\}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-energy}
 G_\nu(t)=t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(M_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Moving-annulus raw-tail criterion]
\label{cor:moving-annulus-tail-criterion}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition},
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 G_\nu(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]
This criterion applies without change to absolutely continuous, singular,
and purely atomic laws.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
On \(A_{t,0}(y)\), the factor
\((1+|y-x/t|^2)^{-a}\) lies between \(2^{-a}\) and \(1\).  On
\(A_{t,k}(y)\), \(k\ge1\), it is comparable, with constants depending only
on \(d\), to \(2^{-k(d+1)}\).  Hence
\[
 c_dM_t^\nu(y)\le V_t^\nu(y)\le C_dM_t^\nu(y).
\]
For each fixed \(c>0\), the ratio \(\Phi(cv)/\Phi(v)\) has positive finite
limits \(c^2\) at zero and \(c\) at infinity and is continuous on
\((0,\infty)\).  Thus fixed multiplicative changes of the argument preserve
two-sided comparability of \(\Phi\).  The conclusion follows from
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Thin-shell aggregation precedes the entropy nonlinearity]
\label{prop:pre-phi-thin-shell-aggregation}
Fix \(d\ge4\), and set
\[
 p=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},\qquad q=\frac{d+5}{d+1}.
\]
Let \(B_n\to\infty\), choose \(R_n\to\infty\), and put
\(a_n=B_nR_n^{-p}\to0\) and
\(t_n=\kappa R_na_n^{1/(d+1)}\), where \(\kappa>0\) is fixed and
sufficiently small.  Let \(K_n\) be integers such that
\[
 B_n^qK_n^{1-q}\longrightarrow0.
\]
There are atoms \(\eta_{n,j}=(a_n/K_n)\delta_{r_{n,j}e_1}\),
\(1\le j\le K_n\), with
\(R_n\le r_{n,j}\le R_n+t_n/10\), for which
\begin{align}
 \sum_{j=1}^{K_n}\sup_{t>0}t^4
 \int\Phi(V_t^{\eta_{n,j}})\dd\Omega_d&\longrightarrow0,
 \label{eq:thin-shell-separated-energy}\\
 t_n^4\int\Phi\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^{K_n}
 V_{t_n}^{\eta_{n,j}}\right)\dd\Omega_d&\longrightarrow+\infty.
 \label{eq:thin-shell-aggregated-energy}
\end{align}
Thus summing shellwise nonlinear energies cannot replace aggregation of the
positive tail potential before applying \(\Phi\).
Here \(V_t^\eta\) denotes the expression on the right side of
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential} with the finite measure \(\eta\) in
place of \(\nu_c\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The inequality \(\Phi(v)\le C_qv^q\), \(v\ge0\), and the exact translation
identity \eqref{eq:critical-lq-exact-translation-norm} show that an atom of
mass \(m\) at radius \(r\) satisfies
\[
 \sup_{t>0}t^4\int\Phi(V_t^{m\delta_{re_1}})\dd\Omega_d
 \le C_dr^4m^q.
\]
Indeed, the potential vanishes for \(t\ge r\); for \(t<r\), the right side
of \eqref{eq:critical-lq-exact-translation-norm} is bounded by
\(C_d\{1+(r/t)^4\}\).  Since \(r_{n,j}\asymp R_n\), summation gives
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^{K_n}\sup_{t>0}t^4
 \int\Phi(V_t^{\eta_{n,j}})\dd\Omega_d
 \le C_dR_n^4a_n^qK_n^{1-q}
 =C_dB_n^qK_n^{1-q},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:thin-shell-separated-energy}.

At scale \(t_n\), all translated centres \(r_{n,j}e_1/t_n\) lie within
distance \(1/10\) of \(R_ne_1/t_n\).  On a fixed sufficiently small ball
about the latter point,
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^{K_n}V_{t_n}^{\eta_{n,j}}
 \ge c_da_n(R_n/t_n)^{d+1}=c_d\kappa^{-(d+1)}.
\]
The \(\Omega_d\)-measure of this ball is bounded below by
\(c_d(t_n/R_n)^{d+1}=c_d\kappa^{d+1}a_n\).  Therefore
\[
 t_n^4\int\Phi\!\left(\sum_jV_{t_n}^{\eta_{n,j}}\right)\dd\Omega_d
 \ge c_dt_n^4a_n
 =c_dR_n^4a_n^q=c_dB_n^q\longrightarrow\infty,
\]
after decreasing \(\kappa\), if necessary, so that the fixed lower value of
the potential is nonzero.  This proves
\eqref{eq:thin-shell-aggregated-energy}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Proxy characterization at the critical vague boundary]
\label{cor:critical-rv-proxy-characterization}
Fix \(d\ge4\), set
\[
 p_d=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},
 \qquad
 \mu_\sigma(\dd\rho,\dd\theta)
 =p_d\rho^{-p_d-1}\dd\rho\,\sigma(\dd\theta),
\]
and let \(\nu\) have mean \(\bar\gamma\), finite covariance \(\Sigma\), and
centred law \(\nu_c\).  Suppose that, for a slowly varying \(L\),
\[
 \frac{r^{p_d}}{L(r)}\nu_c(r\,\mathord\cdot)
 \xrightarrow[r\to\infty]{v}\mu_\sigma
 \quad\text{on }\RR^d\setminus\{0\},
 \qquad
 \ell_L(t):=\int_{R_0}^t\frac{L(s)}s\dd s\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Then
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]
In particular, for the two laws \(\nu_{\rm ac}\) and \(\nu_{\rm cl}\) in
Theorem~\ref{thm:critical-vague-tail-nonidentifiability},
\[
 \mathcal C_{\nu_{\rm ac},\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathcal C_{\nu_{\rm cl},\Sigma}(t)=+\infty.
\]
Thus the spectral measure and slowly varying factor do not determine the
covariance-proxy defect trajectory.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The equivalence is Theorem~\ref{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition};
the regular-variation assumptions specify the critical class but are not
needed for that decomposition.  Its combination with
\eqref{eq:critical-vague-tail-contrasting-entropy} gives the two final
assertions.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Normalization and Cayley--Poisson coordinates}
\label{subsec:entropy-normalization-package}

\subsubsection{Entropy in Cayley coordinates}
\label{subsec:cayley-entropy}

The logarithmic Jacobian of the Cayley coordinate is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:precision-potential}
J_{\log}(x):=-\log J(x),\qquad
J(x):=\upsilon'(x)=\frac{1}{\pi(1+x^{2})},
\end{equation}
equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:precision-potential-expanded}
J_{\log}(x)=\log\pi+\log(1+x^{2}).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Entropy identity in Cayley coordinates]%
\label{thm:cayley-entropy-identity}
Let $X$ be a real-valued random variable with density $f_X$ such that
$h_{\mathrm{diff}}(X)>-\infty$ and $\EE[J_{\log}(X)]<\infty$.  Set
$U:=\upsilon(X)\in\UU$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-entropy-identity}
h_{\mathrm{diff}}(X)
=h_{\mathrm{diff}}(U)+\EE[J_{\log}(X)].
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The density of $U$ is
\[
f_U(u)=f_X(\tan(\pi u))\,\pi(1+\tan^2(\pi u)).
\]
The standard change-of-variables formula for differential entropy
\cite[Chapter~9]{CoverThomas2006} gives
\[
h_{\mathrm{diff}}(X)
=h_{\mathrm{diff}}(U)+\EE\!\left[\log\frac{1}{\abs{\upsilon'(X)}}\right]
=h_{\mathrm{diff}}(U)+\EE[J_{\log}(X)].
\]
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Poisson smoothing}

For $t>0$ and $x\in\RR$ define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-kernel}
P_t(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t}{x^{2}+t^{2}}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Basic identities for the Poisson kernels]%
\label{prop:poisson-identities}
For $s,t>0$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-basic}
P_t(x)=\frac{1}{t}P_1\!\left(\frac{x}{t}\right),
\qquad
\widehat{P_t}(\xi)=e^{-t\abs{\xi}},
\qquad
P_t*P_s=P_{t+s}.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
\omega(\dd x)=P_1(x)\dd x,
\qquad
P_t*\omega=P_{t+1}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The scaling identity is immediate from the definition.  The Fourier
transform of~$P_1$ is $e^{-\abs{\xi}}$
\cite[Chapter~III]{Stein1971}; scaling yields
$\widehat{P_t}(\xi)=e^{-t\abs{\xi}}$.  The convolution law follows by
multiplying Fourier transforms.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Standard facts on the Poisson semigroup]%
\label{prop:poisson-semigroup-law}
For $1\le p\le\infty$ and $t>0$ define
$T_t f:=P_t*f$.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\|T_t f\|_{L^p(\RR)}\le \|f\|_{L^p(\RR)}$ for every $f\in L^p$;
\item $T_{t+s}=T_tT_s$ for all $s,t>0$;
\item for $1\le p<\infty$, $T_t f\to f$ in $L^p(\RR)$ as $t\downarrow 0$;
\item on $L^2(\RR)$, $\widehat{T_t f}(\xi)=e^{-t\abs{\xi}}\widehat f(\xi)$
      and the generator is $-|D|$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $\int_{\RR}P_t(x)\dd x=1$ and $P_t\ge 0$, Young's inequality gives
(i).  The semigroup law is Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-identities}.
Strong continuity for $1\le p<\infty$ follows because $\{P_t\}_{t>0}$ is
an approximate identity.  The $L^2$-multiplier statement is exactly
\eqref{eq:poisson-basic}; the generator follows from
\[
\frac{e^{-t\abs{\xi}}-1}{t}\longrightarrow -\abs{\xi}
\qquad(t\downarrow 0)
\]
in the Fourier representation.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Large-time comparison with a translated Poisson profile}

\begin{proposition}[Second-order approximation]%
\label{prop:poisson-second-order}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean
\[
\bar{\gamma}:=\int_{\RR}\gamma\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
variance
\[
\sigma^{2}:=\int_{\RR}(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})^{2}\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
and finite centred fourth moment.  Define
\[
h_t:=P_t*\nu,
\qquad
g_t(x):=P_t(x-\bar{\gamma}).
\]
Let
\[
U(y):=\frac{3y^{2}-1}{(1+y^{2})^{2}}.
\]
Then there exists a constant $C_\nu>0$ such that for every $t\ge 1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-profile}
\sup_{x\in\RR}\left|
\frac{h_t(x)}{g_t(x)}
-1
-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{t^{2}}U\!\left(\frac{x-\bar{\gamma}}{t}\right)
\right|
\le \frac{C_\nu}{t^{3}}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-l1-rate}
\|h_t-g_t\|_{L^{1}(\RR)}=O(t^{-2}),
\qquad
\|h_t-g_t\|_{L^\infty(\RR)}=O(t^{-3}).
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-kl-rate}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)=O(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}$ and $x=\bar{\gamma}+ty$.  Then
\[
\frac{P_t(x-\gamma)}{g_t(x)}
=\frac{1+y^{2}}{1+(y-X_c/t)^{2}}.
\]
Set $\varepsilon:=X_c/t$.  A direct algebraic computation gives the
exact identity
\begin{align*}
\frac{1+y^{2}}{1+(y-\varepsilon)^{2}}
&=1+\frac{2y}{1+y^{2}}\varepsilon
+\frac{3y^{2}-1}{(1+y^{2})^{2}}\varepsilon^{2} \\
&\quad
+\frac{4y(y^{2}-1)\varepsilon^{3}-(3y^{2}-1)\varepsilon^{4}}
{(1+y^{2})^{2}(1+(y-\varepsilon)^{2})}.
\end{align*}
Since
\[
\frac{4\abs{y}\abs{y^{2}-1}}{(1+y^{2})^{2}}\le 2,
\qquad
\frac{\abs{3y^{2}-1}}{(1+y^{2})^{2}}\le 1,
\]
the remainder is bounded by $2\abs{\varepsilon}^{3}+\abs{\varepsilon}^{4}$
uniformly in~$y$.  Taking expectation and using $\EE[X_c]=0$ yields
\[
\frac{h_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}{g_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}
=1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{t^{2}}U(y)+R_t(y),
\]
with
\[
\sup_{y\in\RR}\abs{R_t(y)}
\le \frac{2\EE[\abs{X_c}^{3}]+\EE[\abs{X_c}^{4}]}{t^{3}}
\]
for $t\ge 1$.  This proves \eqref{eq:poisson-profile}.

The function~$U$ is bounded, so
$\sup_x\abs{h_t(x)/g_t(x)-1}=O(t^{-2})$.  Since $\int_{\RR}g_t=1$,
\[
\|h_t-g_t\|_{L^1}
\le \sup_{x}\abs{\frac{h_t(x)}{g_t(x)}-1}\int_{\RR}g_t(x)\dd x
=O(t^{-2}).
\]
Also $\|g_t\|_{L^\infty}=1/(\pi t)$, so
\[
\|h_t-g_t\|_{L^\infty}
\le \|g_t\|_{L^\infty}\sup_{x}\abs{\frac{h_t(x)}{g_t(x)}-1}
=O(t^{-3}).
\]
Let
\[
\delta_t(x):=\frac{h_t(x)}{g_t(x)}-1.
\]
The bound \eqref{eq:poisson-profile} implies that
$\|\delta_t\|_{L^\infty}=O(t^{-2})$, so for large $t$ one has
$\|\delta_t\|_{L^\infty}\le 1/2$.  Since
$\int g_t\delta_t\dd x=\int(h_t-g_t)\dd x=0$ and
\[
(1+s)\log(1+s)-s\le 2s^{2}
\qquad(\abs{s}\le 1/2),
\]
it follows that
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\int_{\RR}g_t(x)\bigl((1+\delta_t(x))\log(1+\delta_t(x))-\delta_t(x)\bigr)\dd x
\le 2\|\delta_t\|_{L^\infty}^{2}.
\]
Hence \eqref{eq:poisson-kl-rate} holds.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Cayley--Chebyshev modes and finite-order coefficient calculus}
\label{subsec:entropy-coefficient-package}

\subsubsection{Cayley--Chebyshev mode calculus}

The first two results of this subsection are algebraic:
Theorem~\ref{thm:cayley-chebyshev-mode} computes the modes, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-fourier-mode-integral-algorithm} computes their
Cauchy--Haar products.  The finite-moment density and entropy remainders later
identify the evaluated polynomials with Poisson entropy coefficients.

For $\varepsilon\in\RR$ and $y\in\RR$ set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-comparison-kernel}
R_\varepsilon(y):=\frac{1+y^2}{1+(y-\varepsilon)^2}.
\end{equation}
For fixed $y$, the Taylor expansion of $R_\varepsilon(y)$ at
$\varepsilon=0$ defines coefficients $u_n(y)$ through
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-mode-series}
R_\varepsilon(y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u_n(y)\varepsilon^n,
\qquad
|\varepsilon|<\sqrt{1+y^2}.
\end{equation}

The coefficient-notation table below also fixes the phase convention used
throughout Subsections~\ref{subsec:entropy-normalization-package}--%
\ref{subsec:order-eight-coefficient-layer}.  The real variable is always written in
the half-angle coordinate
\[
\theta:=\frac{\vartheta}{2}=\arctan y,\qquad -\pi/2<\theta<\pi/2,
\]
where \(\vartheta=2\arctan y\) is the Cayley phase from
\eqref{eq:cayley-embed}.  Thus \(y=\tan\theta\), and the Laurent coefficient
extraction uses the double-angle circle variable \(z=e^{2\ii\theta}
=e^{\ii\vartheta}\).  An oscillation \(\cos(2k\theta)\) or
\(\sin(2k\theta)\) is therefore a \(z^{\pm k}\) Laurent mode.

\begin{theorem}[Cayley--Chebyshev mode formula]%
\label{thm:cayley-chebyshev-mode}
Let $U_n$ denote the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind.  If
$y=\tan\theta$ with $\theta\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)$, then for every $n\ge 0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-chebyshev-mode}
u_n(\tan\theta)=\cos^n\theta\,U_n(\sin\theta).
\end{equation}
For $n\ge 1$ this is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-chebyshev-trig}
u_n(\tan\theta)
=\cos^{n-1}\theta\,
\sin\!\Bigl((n+1)\Bigl(\frac{\pi}{2}-\theta\Bigr)\Bigr).
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-mode-parity}
u_n(-y)=(-1)^n u_n(y)
\qquad(n\ge 0).
\end{equation}
Moreover, for every integer $m\ge 1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-mode-even-fourier}
u_{2m}(\tan\theta)
=(-1)^m 2^{-(2m-1)}
\sum_{j=0}^{2m-1}\binom{2m-1}{j}\cos(2(j+1)\theta),
\end{equation}
and for every integer $m\ge 0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-mode-odd-fourier}
u_{2m+1}(\tan\theta)
=(-1)^m 2^{-2m}
\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\binom{2m}{j}\sin(2(j+1)\theta).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
With $y=\tan\theta$ one has
\[
R_\varepsilon(\tan\theta)
=\frac{1}{1-2(\varepsilon\cos\theta)\sin\theta+(\varepsilon\cos\theta)^2}.
\]
The Chebyshev generating function
\[
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}U_n(x)r^n=\frac{1}{1-2xr+r^2}
\]
\cite[Chapters~1 and~5]{MasonHandscomb2002}
therefore yields \eqref{eq:cayley-chebyshev-mode} with
$x=\sin\theta$ and $r=\varepsilon\cos\theta$.  Since
\[
U_n(\sin\theta)
=\frac{\sin((n+1)(\pi/2-\theta))}{\cos\theta},
\]
formula \eqref{eq:cayley-chebyshev-trig} follows for $n\ge 1$.  The
parity relation \eqref{eq:cayley-mode-parity} is immediate from
\eqref{eq:cayley-chebyshev-trig}.

For the finite Fourier expansions, write
\[
u_{2m}(\tan\theta)=(-1)^m\cos^{2m-1}\theta\,\cos((2m+1)\theta),
\]
\[
u_{2m+1}(\tan\theta)=(-1)^m\cos^{2m}\theta\,\sin((2m+2)\theta),
\]
and use
\[
1+e^{2\ii\theta}=2e^{\ii\theta}\cos\theta.
\]
Taking real and imaginary parts after the binomial expansion gives
\eqref{eq:cayley-mode-even-fourier} and
\eqref{eq:cayley-mode-odd-fourier}.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Finite-order coefficient algebra}%
\label{subsec:finite-order-coefficient-calculus}

This subsection defines the coefficient algebra.  The display
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient} is formal, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation} evaluates it
only when the required moments exist.  The finite Fourier theorem then reduces
the mode integrals to exact Laurent constant-coefficient extractions.

The coefficient calculus is universal once the Cayley--Chebyshev modes have
been fixed.  For integers $r\ge2$ and $n_1,\dots,n_r\ge2$ define the
Cauchy-weighted mode integral
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-integral-definition}
J(n_1,\dots,n_r):=J_{n_1,\dots,n_r}:=
\int_{\RR}u_{n_1}(y)\cdots u_{n_r}(y)\,\omega(\dd y),
\qquad
\omega(\dd y):=\frac{\dd y}{\pi(1+y^2)}.
\end{equation}
For $k\ge0$ let
$\mathcal P_k:=\QQ[\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_k]$, with the convention
$\mathcal P_0=\mathcal P_1=\QQ$.  In this formal polynomial algebra set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient}
A_k^{\rm form}:=
\sum_{r=2}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\substack{n_1,\dots,n_r\ge2\\ n_1+\cdots+n_r=k}}
\mathfrak m_{n_1}\cdots\mathfrak m_{n_r}\,J(n_1,\dots,n_r),
\end{equation}
with the convention that an empty sum is zero.  Thus
$A_0^{\rm form}=A_1^{\rm form}=A_2^{\rm form}=A_3^{\rm form}=0$.  If a
probability measure $\nu$ has mean $\bar{\gamma}$ and centred moments
$\mu_n=\int(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})^n\dd\nu$ through order~$k$, we write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation}
A_k(\nu):={\rm ev}_{\nu,k}(A_k^{\rm form}),
\qquad
{\rm ev}_{\nu,k}(\mathfrak m_n)=\mu_n,
\end{equation}
for the resulting finite-moment evaluation.

\begin{table}[t]
\caption{Coefficient notation and Cayley phase convention used from this
point onward.}
\label{tab:coefficient-notation}
\centering
\begin{tabular}{p{0.25\textwidth}p{0.64\textwidth}}
\hline
Symbol & Meaning \\
\hline
\(\theta\) & Half-angle Cayley coordinate,
\(-\pi/2<\theta<\pi/2\), with \(y=\tan\theta\).  The Cayley phase in
\eqref{eq:cayley-embed} is \(\vartheta=2\theta\). \\
\(z\) & Laurent/Haar phase \(z=e^{2\ii\theta}=e^{\ii\vartheta}\).
Thus \(\cos(2k\theta)\) and \(\sin(2k\theta)\) are \(z^{\pm k}\) Laurent
modes. \\
\(\omega(\dd y)\) &
\(\displaystyle \frac{\dd y}{\pi(1+y^2)}=\frac{\dd\theta}{\pi}\); under
\(z=e^{2\ii\theta}\) this is normalized Haar measure on \(\TT\). \\
\(Q_n\) & Laurent polynomial defined by
\(Q_n(e^{2\ii\theta})=u_n(\tan\theta)\); its coefficients include the
\(1/2\) and \(1/(2\ii)\) factors from converting cosine and sine to Laurent
modes.  Through order six, if
\(Q_n(z)=\sum_{k=-n}^{n}q_{n,k}z^k\) and \(q_{n,0}=0\), the positive
coefficient vectors are
\(\begin{array}{c|c|c}
n & (q_{n,1},\ldots,q_{n,n}) & q_{n,-k}\ (1\le k\le n)\\
\hline
2 & -\frac14(1,1) & q_{2,k}\\
3 & \frac{\ii}{8}(1,2,1) & -q_{3,k}\\
4 & \frac1{16}(1,3,3,1) & q_{4,k}\\
5 & -\frac{\ii}{32}(1,4,6,4,1) & -q_{5,k}\\
6 & -\frac1{64}(1,5,10,10,5,1) & q_{6,k}.
\end{array}\). \\
\(\mathcal P_k\) & Formal polynomial ring
\(\QQ[\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_k]\).  This algebraic symbol is
unrelated to the Poisson kernels \(P_t\) and to the Cayley logarithmic
Jacobian \(J_{\log}\). \\
\(A_k^{\rm form}\) & Universal polynomial in the formal moment variables
\(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_k\), before any measure is inserted. \\
\(A_k(\nu)\) & Evaluation of \(A_k^{\rm form}\) at the centred moments of
\(\nu\), whenever the moments appearing in that polynomial exist.  When an
entropy-transfer theorem is invoked, the same symbol denotes the identified
large-time entropy coefficient. \\
\(A_{2m}(\nu)\) & Even one-dimensional entropy coefficients in the fixed-law
Poisson KL expansion.  Odd coefficients \(A_{2m-1}(\nu)\) vanish at every
retained order by parity. \\
\(D_j^{(d)}(y;\nu)\) & Degree-\(j\) tensor density layer in the
multidimensional radial Poisson quotient expansion. \\
\(\mathcal A_m^{(d)}(\nu)\) & Multidimensional entropy coefficient obtained
from the tensor layers \(D_j^{(d)}\) in
Proposition~\ref{thm:multidimensional-poisson-entropy-jets}.  The manuscript uses
\(\mathcal A_m^{(d)}\) for the multidimensional coefficients and does not use
an additional \(A_m^{(d)}\) alias in the body. \\
\(\Delta_6(\nu),\mathcal E_8(\nu)\) & The sixth-order two-point defect and
the eighth-order coefficient excess built from the evaluated \(N=4\)
coefficients \(A_6(\nu)\) and \(A_8(\nu)\). \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The phrase ``moments through order~$k$'' is only a uniform sufficient
convention: a particular polynomial $A_k^{\rm form}$ may be evaluated as soon
as the moments that actually occur in it exist.  More concretely, every
entropy monomial has at least two density factors: if
\(n_1+\cdots+n_r=k\), \(r\ge2\), and \(n_j\ge2\), then
\(\max_j n_j\le k-2\).  Thus, for \(k\ge4\), \(A_k^{\rm form}\) involves no
moment above order \(k-2\).  In particular the \(A^{\mathrm{form}}_8\)
polynomial below involves only \(\mu_2,\ldots,\mu_6\).  In the \(N=4\) layer
we use the notation in Table~\ref{tab:coefficient-notation}: \(A_6,A_8\) are
first displayed as finite moment polynomials, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-eighth} is the point at which those same
quantities are identified as the actual \(t^{-6}\) and \(t^{-8}\) entropy
coefficients.  The seventh moment adds only the quantitative remainder
hypothesis.

\begin{theorem}[Universal entropy coefficients]%
\label{thm:universal-entropy-coefficients}
Let $k\ge0$.  In the completed formal Laurent algebra
$\mathcal P_k[[t^{-1}]]$, truncate every product after collecting total
$t$-degree at most~$k$.  If
\[
\delta^{\rm form}_t(y)=\sum_{n=2}^{k}\mathfrak m_n u_n(y)t^{-n},
\]
then the coefficient of $t^{-k}$ in the formal expression
\[
\int_{\RR}\bigl((1+\delta^{\rm form}_t)\log(1+\delta^{\rm form}_t)
-\delta^{\rm form}_t\bigr)\,\dd\omega
\]
is precisely $A_k^{\rm form}$ in
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient}.  Moreover, if $\nu$ is a
probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean $\bar{\gamma}$ and centred moments
$\mu_n=\int(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})^n\dd\nu$ through order~$k$, then the evaluated
coefficient defined by \eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation} is
the explicit finite polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-coefficient-theorem-evaluated}
A_k(\nu)
=
\sum_{r=2}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\substack{n_1,\dots,n_r\ge2\\ n_1+\cdots+n_r=k}}
\mu_{n_1}\cdots\mu_{n_r}\,J(n_1,\dots,n_r),
\end{equation}
and is equivalently the coefficient of $t^{-k}$ in
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-coefficient-theorem-generating}
\int_{\RR}
\left(
\left(1+\sum_{n=2}^{k}\mu_n\,u_n(y)t^{-n}\right)
\log\left(1+\sum_{n=2}^{k}\mu_n\,u_n(y)t^{-n}\right)
-\sum_{n=2}^{k}\mu_n\,u_n(y)t^{-n}
\right)\dd\omega,
\end{equation}
after formal truncation at total $t$-degree~$k$.  Finally,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-odd-coefficients-vanish}
A_k^{\rm form}=0\qquad\text{whenever }k\text{ is odd}.
\end{equation}
Consequently $A_k(\nu)=0$ for odd~$k$ whenever the moments required to evaluate
$A_k(\nu)$ exist.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The Taylor series
\[
(1+s)\log(1+s)-s
=\sum_{r=2}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}s^r
\]
has no constant or linear term.  In the formal substitution for
$\delta_t^{\rm form}$, a monomial in the $r$th power has the form
\[
\mathfrak m_{n_1}\cdots\mathfrak m_{n_r}
u_{n_1}(y)\cdots u_{n_r}(y)t^{-(n_1+\cdots+n_r)},
\qquad n_j\ge2.
\]
For fixed total degree~$k$ there are only finitely many such monomials, and
necessarily $2\le r\le\lfloor k/2\rfloor$.  Integrating the degree-$k$ part
against~$\omega$ gives exactly \eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient}.
Evaluation by ${\rm ev}_{\nu,k}$ is a homomorphism from the finite polynomial
algebra $\mathcal P_k$ to~$\RR$.  Applying it to
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient} gives
\eqref{eq:universal-coefficient-theorem-evaluated}, and applying it
coefficient-by-coefficient to the finite truncated formal generating identity
gives \eqref{eq:universal-coefficient-theorem-generating}.  This evaluation
uses only the centred moments through order~$k$.

It remains to prove the odd cancellation.  By
\eqref{eq:cayley-mode-parity}, $u_n(-y)=(-1)^n u_n(y)$, while
$\omega(\dd y)$ is even.  Hence the integrand in
$J(n_1,\dots,n_r)$ is odd whenever $n_1+\cdots+n_r$ is odd, and then the
integral vanishes.  Every summand in $A_k^{\rm form}$ has total index~$k$, so
all summands vanish when $k$ is odd.  Evaluating this formal identity gives
the asserted odd vanishing of $A_k(\nu)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Universal entropy coefficients: finite-moment layer]%
\label{prop:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation}
Let $k\ge0$, and let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean
$\bar{\gamma}$ and with all centred moments that occur in
$A_k^{\rm form}$.  Then the quantity $A_k(\nu)$ in
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation} is a finite polynomial in
the actual centred moments of~$\nu$ and in the universal mode integrals
\eqref{eq:mode-integral-definition}.  For \(k\ge4\) this requires no moment
above order \(k-2\).  If $k$ is odd, then $A_k(\nu)=0$.  No measure-level
value of $A_k(\nu)$ is asserted when the required moments do not exist.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Evaluation by ${\rm ev}_{\nu,k}$ is a homomorphism from the finite polynomial
algebra $\mathcal P_k$ to~$\RR$.  Applying it to the identity of
Theorem~\ref{thm:universal-entropy-coefficients} gives exactly
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation}.  The odd cancellation is
the image of \eqref{eq:universal-odd-coefficients-vanish} under the same
evaluation map.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:universal-entropy-coefficients} gives the formal coefficient
identity, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation} gives its
finite-moment evaluation.  The large-time expansion is the finite-moment
statement of Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}.

\begin{theorem}[Finite Fourier algorithm for mode integrals]%
\label{thm:finite-fourier-mode-integral-algorithm}
For each $n\ge1$ define a Laurent polynomial $Q_n$ on the unit circle by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-laurent-polynomial-definition}
Q_n(e^{2\ii\theta}):=u_n(\tan\theta),
\qquad -\pi/2<\theta<\pi/2.
\end{equation}
This definition includes the Laurent factors coming from the passage from
trigonometric functions to the variable \(z=e^{2\ii\theta}\).
Explicitly, for $m\ge1$ and $m\ge0$, respectively,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-laurent-even}
Q_{2m}(z)=(-1)^m2^{-2m}
\sum_{j=0}^{2m-1}\binom{2m-1}{j}
\bigl(z^{j+1}+z^{-(j+1)}\bigr),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-laurent-odd}
Q_{2m+1}(z)=(-1)^m\frac{2^{-2m}}{2\ii}
\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\binom{2m}{j}
\bigl(z^{j+1}-z^{-(j+1)}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every $r\ge2$ and $n_1,\dots,n_r\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-integral-constant-term}
J(n_1,\dots,n_r)
=\bigl[z^0\bigr]\prod_{a=1}^{r}Q_{n_a}(z),
\end{equation}
where $[z^0]$ denotes constant Laurent coefficient.  Thus every formal
universal coefficient $A_k^{\rm form}$, and every evaluated coefficient
$A_k(\nu)$ when the required moments exist, is obtained by finitely many
Laurent-polynomial multiplications and constant-coefficient extractions.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Equations \eqref{eq:mode-laurent-even} and \eqref{eq:mode-laurent-odd} are
exactly the finite Fourier formulas
\eqref{eq:cayley-mode-even-fourier} and
\eqref{eq:cayley-mode-odd-fourier} after writing
$z=e^{2\ii\theta}$,
\[
\cos(2\ell\theta)=\frac{z^\ell+z^{-\ell}}2,
\qquad
\sin(2\ell\theta)=\frac{z^\ell-z^{-\ell}}{2\ii}.
\]
Thus, for instance, the Fourier term
\(-\frac12(\cos2\theta+\cos4\theta)\) gives
\(Q_2(z)=-\frac14(z+z^{-1}+z^2+z^{-2})\), so \(Q_2(1)=-1\).
The change of variables $y=\tan\theta$ gives
\[
\omega(\dd y)=\frac{\dd\theta}{\pi}
\qquad(-\pi/2<\theta<\pi/2),
\]
and $z=e^{2\ii\theta}$ pushes $\dd\theta/\pi$ to normalized Haar measure
on~$\TT$.  Hence $J(n_1,\dots,n_r)$ is the Haar integral of the Laurent
polynomial $\prod_a Q_{n_a}$, which is its constant coefficient.  The last
assertion follows by substituting
\eqref{eq:mode-integral-constant-term} into
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Finite mode truncations]%
\label{prop:cayley-mode-remainder}
For every integer $N\ge 1$ there exists a rational function
$v_N:\RR^2\to\RR$, bounded on~$\RR^2$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-mode-remainder}
R_\varepsilon(y)
=\sum_{n=0}^{N}u_n(y)\varepsilon^n+\varepsilon^{N+1}v_N(y,\varepsilon)
\qquad(y,\varepsilon\in\RR).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For every $x$ and $r$ one has the finite Chebyshev-sum identity
\[
\sum_{n=0}^{N}U_n(x)r^n
=\frac{1-r^{N+1}U_{N+1}(x)+r^{N+2}U_N(x)}{1-2xr+r^2}.
\]
\cite[Chapter~5]{MasonHandscomb2002}
Substituting $x=\sin\theta$ and $r=\varepsilon\cos\theta$ gives
\eqref{eq:cayley-mode-remainder} with
\[
v_N(\tan\theta,\varepsilon)
=\frac{\cos^{N+1}\theta\,U_{N+1}(\sin\theta)
-\varepsilon\cos^{N+2}\theta\,U_N(\sin\theta)}
{1-2(\varepsilon\cos\theta)\sin\theta+(\varepsilon\cos\theta)^2}.
\]
Equivalently, since
\[
1-2(\varepsilon\cos\theta)\sin\theta
   +(\varepsilon\cos\theta)^2
=\frac{1+(\tan\theta-\varepsilon)^2}{1+\tan^2\theta},
\]
we have
\[
v_N(y,\varepsilon)
=\frac{(1+y^2)u_{N+1}(y)-\varepsilon u_N(y)}
       {1+(y-\varepsilon)^2},
\]
so $v_N$ is rational in $(y,\varepsilon)$.

It remains to prove boundedness.  Put
$c=\cos\theta$, $s=\sin\theta$, and $d=\tan\theta-\varepsilon$.  Then
$\varepsilon c=s-dc$, and the preceding trigonometric formula becomes
\[
v_N(\tan\theta,\varepsilon)
=\frac{c^{N-1}\bigl(U_{N+1}(s)-sU_N(s)+dc\,U_N(s)\bigr)}
       {1+d^2}.
\]
For $|s|\le1$, the polynomials $U_N$ and $U_{N+1}$ are bounded by
constants depending only on~$N$.  Since $N\ge1$, $|c|^{N-1}\le1$, and
\[
\frac{|d|\,|c|^N}{1+d^2}\le \frac{|d|}{1+d^2}\le \frac12,
\]
the right-hand side is bounded uniformly in $\theta$ and~$\varepsilon$.
This proves that $v_N$ is bounded on~$\RR^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Closed envelopes for Cayley mode constants]%
\label{lem:closed-cayley-mode-constant-envelopes}
For every integer $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-mode-sup-bound}
\|u_n\|_{L^\infty(\RR)}\le n+1 .
\end{equation}
For every integer $L\ge1$, the rational remainder in
Proposition~\ref{prop:cayley-mode-remainder} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-vL-sup-bound}
\|v_L\|_{L^\infty(\RR^2)}\le \frac{5L+7}{2}.
\end{equation}
Consequently the constants in
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-moment-density-remainder} may be dominated by the
closed expressions
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-density-constant-envelopes}
B_L\le \widehat B_L:=L+1,\qquad
V_L\le \widehat V_L:=\frac{5L+7}{2},\qquad
C_L\le \widehat C_L:=\max\!\left\{\frac{5L+7}{2},\,6+(L+1)^2\right\}.
\end{equation}
Thus, if $\nu$ has finite centred $(L+\eta)$th absolute moment and
$0<\eta\le1$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-law-density-constant-envelope}
C_{\nu,L,\eta}
\le
\widehat C_{\nu,L,\eta}
:=2\widehat C_L\int_{\RR}|\gamma-\bar{\gamma}|^{L+\eta}\dd\nu(\gamma),
\end{equation}
and the corresponding smallness constants may be chosen with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-law-smallness-envelope}
S_{\nu,L,\eta}\le
\widehat S_{\nu,L,\eta}:=
\widehat B_L\sum_{n=2}^{L}|\mu_n|+\widehat C_{\nu,L,\eta},
\qquad
T_{\nu,L,\eta}\le
\widehat T_{\nu,L,\eta}:=
\max\{1,(2\widehat S_{\nu,L,\eta})^{1/2}\}.
\end{equation}
For the \(N=4\) entropy layer used later, \(L=6\) and \(\eta=1\).  Writing
\(M_7(\nu):=\EE|X_c|^7\), the preceding closed envelopes give the directly
checkable choices
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-n4-density-envelope}
B_6\le7,\qquad V_6\le\frac{37}{2},\qquad C_6\le55,\qquad
C_{\nu,6,1}\le110M_7(\nu),
\end{equation}


 succeeded in 170ms:
\[
S_{\nu,6,1}\le7\sum_{n=2}^{6}|\mu_n|+110M_7(\nu),
\qquad
T_{\nu,6,1}\le
\max\!\left\{1,\left(14\sum_{n=2}^{6}|\mu_n|+220M_7(\nu)\right)^{1/2}\right\}.
\]
  In particular, on any centred $(L+\eta)$-moment ball
  $\EE|X_c|^{L+\eta}\le M$, Lyapunov's inequality gives the fully closed
  choices
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-moment-ball-density-envelope}
  C_{\nu,L,\eta}\le 2\widehat C_LM,\qquad
  S_{\nu,L,\eta}\le
  \widehat B_L\sum_{n=2}^{L}M^{n/(L+\eta)}+2\widehat C_LM,
  \end{equation}
  and
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:closed-moment-ball-threshold-envelope}
  T_{\nu,L,\eta}\le
  \max\!\left\{1,\left(2\widehat B_L\sum_{n=2}^{L}M^{n/(L+\eta)}
  +4\widehat C_LM\right)^{1/2}\right\}.
  \end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The Chebyshev representation
\eqref{eq:cayley-chebyshev-mode} gives
\[
|u_n(\tan\theta)|
\le |\cos\theta|^n\sup_{|x|\le1}|U_n(x)|
\le n+1,
\]
because $\sup_{|x|\le1}|U_n(x)|=n+1$.  This proves
\eqref{eq:closed-mode-sup-bound}.

For the remainder write, as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:cayley-mode-remainder},
$s=\sin\theta$, $c=\cos\theta$, and $d=\tan\theta-\varepsilon$.  Then
\[
v_L(\tan\theta,\varepsilon)
=\frac{c^{L-1}\bigl(U_{L+1}(s)-sU_L(s)+dc\,U_L(s)\bigr)}
       {1+d^2}.
\]
Since $|c|\le1$, $|s|\le1$,
$\sup_{|x|\le1}|U_L(x)|=L+1$, and
$\sup_{|x|\le1}|U_{L+1}(x)|=L+2$,
\[
|v_L(\tan\theta,\varepsilon)|
\le (L+2)+(L+1)
 +(L+1)\frac{|d|\,|c|^L}{1+d^2}
\le 2L+3+\frac{L+1}{2}
=\frac{5L+7}{2}.
\]
This is uniform in $\theta$ and $\varepsilon$, proving
\eqref{eq:closed-vL-sup-bound}.  The bounds for $B_L,V_L,C_L$ follow
directly from their definitions in
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-moment-density-remainder}; substituting those
dominants into the displayed definitions of
$C_{\nu,L,\eta}$, $S_{\nu,L,\eta}$, and $T_{\nu,L,\eta}$ gives
\eqref{eq:closed-law-density-constant-envelope} and
\eqref{eq:closed-law-smallness-envelope}.
If $\EE|X_c|^{L+\eta}\le M$, then
$|\mu_n|\le M^{n/(L+\eta)}$ for $2\le n\le L$, so the two moment-ball
displays follow from the same substitutions.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Tail-uniform Cayley mode remainder]%
\label{lem:tail-uniform-cayley-mode-remainder}
Let $N\ge1$, let $X$ be a real random variable with
$\EE|X|^{N+1}<\infty$, and let
\[
\mathcal R_{N,t}(y):=
\EE\!\left[
R_{X/t}(y)-\sum_{n=0}^{N}u_n(y)\frac{X^n}{t^n}
\right]
\qquad(t>0).
\]
Then there is a constant $M_N<\infty$, depending only on~$N$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tail-uniform-cayley-mode-remainder}
\sup_{y\in\RR}|\mathcal R_{N,t}(y)|
\;+\;\|\mathcal R_{N,t}\|_{L^1(\omega)}
\le
M_N\,\EE|X|^{N+1}\,t^{-(N+1)},
\end{equation}
where $\omega(\dd y)=\pi^{-1}(1+y^2)^{-1}\dd y$.  Thus the remainder is
uniform in the phase variable after integration over the full, unbounded
tail of~$X$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:cayley-mode-remainder},
\[
R_{X/t}(y)-\sum_{n=0}^{N}u_n(y)\frac{X^n}{t^n}
=\frac{X^{N+1}}{t^{N+1}}v_N(y,X/t)
\]
for every $y$ and every value of~$X$.  Let
$M_N:=\|v_N\|_{L^\infty(\RR^2)}$.  Taking expectations gives
\[
|\mathcal R_{N,t}(y)|
\le M_N\,\EE|X|^{N+1}\,t^{-(N+1)}
\qquad(y\in\RR).
\]
This is the asserted uniform estimate.  Since $\omega$ is a probability
measure, the same bound also gives the displayed $L^1(\omega)$ estimate.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Density quotient expansion with finite-moment remainder]%
\label{thm:finite-moment-density-remainder}
Let $L\ge2$, let $0<\eta\le1$, and let $\nu$ be a probability measure
on~$\RR$ with mean $\bar{\gamma}$ and finite centred $(L+\eta)$th absolute
moment.  Put
$X_c=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}$,
$\mu_n=\int X_c^n\dd\nu(\gamma)$, and
\[
\delta_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty):=\frac{(P_t*\nu)(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}
{P_t(ty)}-1.
\]
Let
\[
B_L:=\max_{0\le n\le L}\|u_n\|_\infty,
\qquad
V_L:=\|v_L\|_\infty,
\qquad
C_L:=\max\{V_L,\,6+(L+1)B_L\}.
\]
Then \eqref{eq:finite-moment-density-remainder} holds with
$C_{\nu,L,\eta}=2C_L\EE|X_c|^{L+\eta}$.  Moreover, with
\[
S_{\nu,L,\eta}:=
B_L\sum_{n=2}^{L}|\mu_n|+C_{\nu,L,\eta},
\qquad
T_{\nu,L,\eta}:=\max\{1,(2S_{\nu,L,\eta})^{1/2}\},
\]
one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-moment-density-remainder}
\delta_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)
=\sum_{n=2}^{L}\mu_n u_n(y)t^{-n}+\rho_{L,t}(y),
\qquad
\sup_y|\rho_{L,t}(y)|+\|\rho_{L,t}\|_{L^1(\omega)}
\le C_{\nu,L,\eta}t^{-(L+\eta)},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-moment-density-smallness}
\sup_y|\delta_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)|\le S_{\nu,L,\eta}t^{-2},
\qquad t\ge1,
\end{equation}
with $\sup_y|\delta_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)|\le1/2$ for
$t\ge T_{\nu,L,\eta}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
With $x=\bar{\gamma}+ty$ and $g_t(x)=P_t(x-\bar{\gamma})=P_t(ty)$,
\[
\frac{(P_t*\nu)(x)}{g_t(x)}
=\int_{\RR}R_{X_c/t}(y)\dd\nu(\gamma).
\]
For $|\varepsilon|\le1$, Proposition~\ref{prop:cayley-mode-remainder} and
the boundedness of $v_L$ give
\[
\left|R_\varepsilon(y)-\sum_{n=0}^{L}u_n(y)\varepsilon^n\right|
    \le V_L |\varepsilon|^{L+1}
    \le V_L |\varepsilon|^{L+\eta}.
\]
For $|\varepsilon|>1$, write $y=z+\varepsilon$.  Then
\[
R_\varepsilon(y)=\frac{1+(z+\varepsilon)^2}{1+z^2}
\le 3(1+\varepsilon^2)
\le 6|\varepsilon|^{L+\eta},
\]
uniformly in~$z$, because $L+\eta\ge2$.  Also
\[
\left|\sum_{n=0}^{L}u_n(y)\varepsilon^n\right|
\le (L+1)B_L|\varepsilon|^L
\le (L+1)B_L|\varepsilon|^{L+\eta}.
\]
Thus the pointwise remainder is bounded by
$C_L|\varepsilon|^{L+\eta}$ uniformly in~$y$.
Taking $\varepsilon=X_c/t$ and expectations yields the displayed
$t^{-(L+\eta)}$ bound in both $L^\infty$ and $L^1(\omega)$, since $\omega$
has total mass one.  Since $\mu_0=1$ and $\mu_1=0$, subtracting the
constant term gives \eqref{eq:finite-moment-density-remainder}.  Each
$u_n$ is bounded by Theorem~\ref{thm:cayley-chebyshev-mode}; hence the
finite sum begins at order~$t^{-2}$.  The displayed definition of
$S_{\nu,L,\eta}$ therefore gives
\eqref{eq:finite-moment-density-smallness}, because $L+\eta\ge2$ and
$t\ge1$.  The displayed choice of $T_{\nu,L,\eta}$ gives the final
smallness assertion.
\end{proof}

For later use we record the first modes explicitly:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-first-modes}
u_2(y)=\frac{3y^2-1}{(1+y^2)^2},
\qquad
u_3(y)=\frac{4y(y^2-1)}{(1+y^2)^3},
\qquad
u_4(y)=\frac{5y^4-10y^2+1}{(1+y^2)^4},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-next-modes}
u_5(y)=\frac{6y^5-20y^3+6y}{(1+y^2)^5},
\qquad
u_6(y)=\frac{7y^6-35y^4+21y^2-1}{(1+y^2)^6}.
\end{equation}

The trigonometric coefficients below are ordinary Haar-Fourier coefficients of
the circle characters in the Cayley phase coordinate.  The eighth-order
entropy arithmetic itself is not repeated here; its single finite Laurent
certificate is Lemma~\ref{lem:eighth-order-coefficient-arithmetic}.

\begin{proposition}[Poisson--Cauchy moment inversion by phase modes]%
\label{prop:poisson-cauchy-moment-inversion-by-phase-modes}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean~$\bar{\gamma}$ and
finite centred fifth absolute moment.  Put
\[
X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma},\qquad
\mu_n:=\int_{\RR}X_c^n\,\dd\nu(\gamma),\qquad
\sigma^2:=\mu_2,
\]
and let $h_t:=P_t*\nu$ and $g_t(x):=P_t(x-\bar{\gamma})$.  For
$\theta\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)$ define the phase profile
\[
F_t(\theta):=
\frac{h_t(\bar{\gamma}+t\tan\theta)}
     {g_t(\bar{\gamma}+t\tan\theta)}-1.
\]
For $k\ge1$ set
\[
\mathcal C_k(t):=
2\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}F_t(\theta)\cos(2k\theta)\,\frac{\dd\theta}{\pi},
\qquad
\mathcal S_k(t):=
2\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}F_t(\theta)\sin(2k\theta)\,\frac{\dd\theta}{\pi}.
\]
Then
\[
\sigma^2=\lim_{t\to\infty}-2t^2\bigl(\mathcal C_1(t)-\mathcal C_4(t)\bigr),
\qquad
\mu_3=\lim_{t\to\infty}-4t^3\mathcal S_3(t),
\qquad
\mu_4=\lim_{t\to\infty}8t^4\mathcal C_4(t).
\]
More precisely,
\[
\mathcal C_1(t)-\mathcal C_4(t)
=-\frac{\sigma^2}{2t^2}+O(t^{-5}),\qquad
\mathcal S_3(t)=-\frac{\mu_3}{4t^3}+O(t^{-5}),
\qquad
\mathcal C_4(t)=\frac{\mu_4}{8t^4}+O(t^{-5}).
\]
The $O(t^{-5})$ term in the middle estimate is an actual fifth-order
remainder: when the fifth centred moment is finite it contains the
$\sin(6\theta)$ coefficient of $u_5$, and no coefficient-free cancellation at
order $t^{-5}$ is asserted.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $y=\tan\theta$.  For $x=\bar{\gamma}+ty$ one has
\[
\frac{P_t(x-\gamma)}{P_t(x-\bar{\gamma})}
=\frac{1+y^2}{1+(y-X_c/t)^2}
=R_{X_c/t}(y).
\]
Therefore
\[
F_t(\theta)=\int_{\RR}\bigl(R_{X_c/t}(\tan\theta)-1\bigr)
\,\dd\nu(\gamma).
\]
Proposition~\ref{prop:cayley-mode-remainder} gives the pointwise
order-four identity
\[
R_{X_c/t}(y)-1
=\sum_{n=1}^{4}u_n(y)\frac{X_c^n}{t^n}
+\frac{X_c^5}{t^5}v_4(y,X_c/t).
\]
Taking $\nu$-expectations and using $\mu_1=0$ gives, uniformly in
$\theta$,
\[
F_t(\theta)
=\frac{\sigma^2}{t^2}u_2(\tan\theta)
+\frac{\mu_3}{t^3}u_3(\tan\theta)
+\frac{\mu_4}{t^4}u_4(\tan\theta)
+O(t^{-5}).
\]
The finite fifth absolute moment is used only through
Lemma~\ref{lem:tail-uniform-cayley-mode-remainder}, with $X=X_c$ and
truncation order~$4$, to bound the last expectation uniformly.
Thus the $O(t^{-5})$ term has not been discarded coefficientwise; it is the
full order-five tail produced by $v_4$.  If one instead expands through
$u_5$, the formula
\[
u_5(\tan\theta)=\frac1{16}\bigl(\sin2\theta+4\sin4\theta
+6\sin6\theta+4\sin8\theta+\sin10\theta\bigr)
\]
shows explicitly that the $\mathcal S_3$ channel has a fifth-order
contribution $3\mu_5/(8t^5)$ whenever $\mu_5$ is finite; the proposition uses
only the leading coefficient needed for the moment limit.

The first three nonconstant modes have the finite phase-Fourier
expansions
\[
u_2(\tan\theta)=-\frac12\bigl(\cos2\theta+\cos4\theta\bigr),
\]
\[
u_3(\tan\theta)
=-\frac14\bigl(\sin2\theta+2\sin4\theta+\sin6\theta\bigr),
\]
\[
u_4(\tan\theta)
=\frac18\bigl(\cos2\theta+3\cos4\theta+3\cos6\theta+\cos8\theta\bigr),
\]
which are the cases $n=2,3,4$ of
Theorem~\ref{thm:cayley-chebyshev-mode}.  The functions
$\cos(2k\theta)$ and $\sin(2k\theta)$ are orthogonal on
$(-\pi/2,\pi/2)$, and the prefactor $2/\pi$ in the definitions of
$\mathcal C_k$ and $\mathcal S_k$ extracts the corresponding coefficient.
Thus the preceding uniform expansion gives
\[
\mathcal C_1(t)
=-\frac{\sigma^2}{2t^2}+\frac{\mu_4}{8t^4}+O(t^{-5}),
\qquad
\mathcal C_4(t)=\frac{\mu_4}{8t^4}+O(t^{-5}),
\]
and
\[
\mathcal S_3(t)=-\frac{\mu_3}{4t^3}+O(t^{-5}).
\]
The last displayed remainder includes the possible fifth-order sine-mode
contribution just identified.
Subtracting the two cosine coefficients and multiplying by the displayed
powers of~$t$ proves the three inversion formulas.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Mode parity cancellation]\label{lem:mode-parity-vanish}
For integers $n_1,\dots,n_k\ge 1$ let
\[
J_{n_1,\dots,n_k}:=
\int_{\RR}
\frac{u_{n_1}(y)\cdots u_{n_k}(y)}{\pi(1+y^2)}\,\dd y.
\]
Then $J_{n_1,\dots,n_k}=0$ whenever $n_1+\cdots+n_k$ is odd.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
From \eqref{eq:cayley-mode-parity}, $u_n(-y)=(-1)^n u_n(y)$ for every
$n\ge1$, while $(1+y^2)^{-1}$ is even.  Hence the integrand is odd if
$n_1+\cdots+n_k$ is odd, so the integral vanishes.
\end{proof}
