% sec_verified_A2_results.tex -- independently verified structural extensions
\section{Mode Closure, Gaussian Compression, and Dimensional Thresholds}
\label{sec:verified-A2-results}

This section records extensions which do not follow by merely evaluating
further members of the coefficient hierarchy.  The comparison law is allowed
to move, and the multidimensional argument is carried out in the topology
actually needed for the leading entropy coefficient.  Throughout this
section, Gaussian quadrature refers to quadrature for a probability measure
on the real line, not to Gaussian smoothing.

\subsection{The first Cayley channel}

Let \(X=\gamma-\bar\gamma\), and, for \(t>0\), define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-cayley-channel-definition}
 a_t(X):=\frac{\ii X}{2t-\ii X},
 \qquad c_k(t):=\EE[a_t(X)^k],\quad k\ge1.
\end{equation}
The following statement concerns the centred law of \(X\).  In particular,
it is invariant under a simultaneous translation of the original law and
its matched reference.

\begin{theorem}[First-channel closure and the even-moment hierarchy]
\label{thm:first-cayley-mode-closure}
Let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR\) with finite mean, and use the
Cayley coordinate
\[
 z=\frac{1+\ii y}{1-\ii y}\in\TT\setminus\{-1\}.
\]
Then, for every \(t>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-exact-fourier-series}
 \frac{(P_t*\nu)(\bar\gamma+ty)}{P_t(ty)}
 =1+\sum_{k\ge1}
 \bigl(\overline{c_k(t)}z^k+c_k(t)z^{-k}\bigr).
\end{equation}
The series converges pointwise for every \(y\in\RR\), locally uniformly in
\(y\), and in \(L^2(\omega)\) whenever \(\EE X^2<\infty\).  Its coefficients
satisfy the exact differential closure
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-differential-closure}
 c_{k+1}(t)=-c_k(t)-\frac{t}{k}c_k'(t),
 \qquad k\ge1.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the scale trajectory \(c_1\), with its successive derivatives,
determines the matched quotient of the centred law at every scale.  Under
finite variance one also has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-chi-square-parseval}
 \chi^2\!\left(P_t*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 P_t(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma)\right)
 =2\sum_{k\ge1}|c_k(t)|^2.
\end{equation}
If \(\varphi_X(s)=\EE e^{\ii sX}\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-laplace-resolvent}
 \frac{1+c_1(t)}{2t}
 =\EE\frac1{2t-\ii X}
 =\int_0^\infty e^{-2ts}\varphi_X(s)\,\dd s.
\end{equation}
Thus the full scale trajectory identifies the centred law by the classical
uniqueness of the Laplace and characteristic transforms.  It identifies the
untranslated law \(\nu\) only when \(\bar\gamma\) is supplied separately.

Moreover, if \(n\ge1\) and \(\mu_{2j}=\EE X^{2j}<\infty\) for
\(1\le j<n\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-even-moment-remainder}
\begin{aligned}
 &(-1)^{n-1}4^nt^{2n}
 \left[-\Re c_1(t)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}
 (-1)^{j-1}\frac{\mu_{2j}}{4^jt^{2j}}\right]\\
 &\hspace{32mm}
 =\EE\!\left[\frac{X^{2n}}{1+X^2/(4t^2)}\right]
 \uparrow\EE|X|^{2n}
 \quad(t\to\infty),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the limit is permitted to be \(+\infty\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix a deterministic translation \(\varepsilon=X/t\).
The pole calculation in
Lemma~\ref{lem:second-mode-cauchy-translate-transform} gives
\[
 R_\varepsilon(y)
 =1+\sum_{k\ge1}\bigl(\overline{a_t(X)}^{\,k}z^k
 +a_t(X)^kz^{-k}\bigr).
\]
Indeed, the interior pole in that calculation is
\(-\varepsilon/(\varepsilon+2\ii)=\ii X/(2t-\ii X)\), while the inverse of
the exterior pole is its complex conjugate.  For finite \(X\),
\(|a_t(X)|<1\).  On a compact subset of
\(\TT\setminus\{-1\}\), the denominators in the two geometric remainders
are bounded away from zero uniformly over the compactified parameter line
\(X\in\RR\cup\{\infty\}\); the only boundary value of \(a_t(X)\) is
\(-1\).  Dominated convergence therefore permits expectation of the finite
partial sums and passage to the limit, proving
\eqref{eq:first-channel-exact-fourier-series} and the asserted local
uniformity.

Pointwise differentiation gives
\[
 a_t(X)^{k+1}=-a_t(X)^k-\frac{t}{k}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd t}a_t(X)^k.
\]
The derivative is integrable locally uniformly in \(t\), because the same
identity bounds its absolute value by \(2k/t\).  Differentiation under the
expectation proves \eqref{eq:first-channel-differential-closure}.

Also \(1+a_t(X)=2t/(2t-\ii X)\).  The identity
\[
 \frac1{2t-\ii X}=\int_0^\infty e^{-(2t-\ii X)s}\,\dd s
\]
and Fubini's theorem prove \eqref{eq:first-channel-laplace-resolvent}.

If \(\EE X^2<\infty\), Jensen's inequality and the geometric series give
\[
 \sum_{k\ge1}|c_k(t)|^2
 \le\EE\sum_{k\ge1}|a_t(X)|^{2k}
 =\frac{\EE X^2}{4t^2}<\infty.
\]
Thus the Fourier series converges in \(L^2(\omega)\), where it agrees with
the quotient already identified pointwise.  Parseval's identity gives
\eqref{eq:first-channel-chi-square-parseval}.

Finally,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-real-part}
 -\Re c_1(t)=\EE\frac{X^2}{4t^2+X^2}.
\end{equation}
For \(q\ge0\), finite geometric division yields
\[
 \frac q{1+q}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{j-1}q^j
 +(-1)^{n-1}\frac{q^n}{1+q}.
\]
Apply this identity with \(q=X^2/(4t^2)\), take expectations, and multiply
by \(4^nt^{2n}\).  The right side of
\eqref{eq:first-channel-even-moment-remainder} increases pointwise with
\(t\), so monotone convergence proves the final assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The infinite critical-moment boundary is not spectrally identifiable}

The finite critical-moment theorem below cannot be extended by replacing its
moment hypothesis with vague multivariate regular variation at the same
index.  We use the standard measure formulation of multivariate regular
variation on \(\RR^d\setminus\{0\}\), as in
Hult--Lindskog~\cite{HultLindskog2006RegularVariation}.  For a probability
measure \(\sigma\) on \(S^{d-1}\) and \(p>0\), write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-rv-tail-measure}
 \mu_\sigma(\dd\rho,\dd\theta)
 =p\rho^{-p-1}\dd\rho\,\sigma(\dd\theta).
\end{equation}

We first record a lower bound which requires no regular-variation
hypothesis.

\begin{lemma}[Finite-covariance Fatou lower bound]
\label{lem:finite-covariance-fatou-lower-bound}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a centred probability law on \(\RR^d\)
with finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Then, with the relative entropy
understood in the extended sense,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-covariance-fatou-lower-bound}
 \liminf_{t\to\infty}t^4H_d(t)\ge \mathcal Q_d(\Sigma).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix \(y\in\RR^d\).  Taylor's formula near the origin, followed by the
elementary large-\(z\) bound, gives
\[
 \left|F_y(z)-1-D_zF_y(0)z\right|\le C_y|z|^2,
 \qquad z\in\RR^d.
\]
Indeed, the assertion is the usual second-order Taylor bound for
\(|z|\le1\); for \(|z|>1\), \(F_y(z)\le(1+|y|^2)^{(d+1)/2}\), while the
subtracted affine function is \(O_y(1+|z|)\).  Centering and dominated
convergence therefore imply
\[
 t^2\delta_t^{(d)}(y)\longrightarrow b_\Sigma(y).
\]
Since \(\Phi\ge0\) on \([-1,\infty)\) and
\(t^4\Phi(\delta_t^{(d)}(y))\to b_\Sigma(y)^2/2\), Fatou's lemma yields
\[
 \liminf_{t\to\infty}t^4H_d(t)
 \ge \frac12\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma),
\]
where the last identity is Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Critical vague tails do not identify the leading entropy]
\label{thm:critical-vague-tail-nonidentifiability}
Fix \(d\ge4\), and set
\[
 p=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},\qquad
 q=\frac{d+5}{d+1}=1+\frac4{d+1};
\]
thus \(pq=4\).  Let \(\sigma\) be normalized surface measure on
\(S^{d-1}\), and let \(L(r)=\log r\).  For every positive-definite matrix
\(\Sigma\), there are two centred probability laws
\(\nu_{\rm ac}\) and \(\nu_{\rm cl}\) with covariance \(\Sigma\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-vague-tail-common-limit}
 \frac{r^p}{\log r}\,\nu_\bullet(r\,\mathord\cdot)
 \xrightarrow[r\to\infty]{v}\mu_\sigma
 \quad\text{on }\RR^d\setminus\{0\},
 \qquad \bullet\in\{\mathrm{ac},\mathrm{cl}\},
\end{equation}
but
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-vague-tail-contrasting-entropy}
 t^4H_{d,{\rm ac}}(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma),
 \qquad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H_{d,{\rm cl}}(t)=+\infty.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the spectral measure and the slowly varying factor do not
determine whether the covariance lower bound is an asymptotic equality,
even when the spectral measure is smooth, everywhere nondegenerate, and
antipodally symmetric.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We construct the tails first and then complete them on a bounded set.  Choose
\(s_1\) large and put \(R_0=e^{s_1}\).  The diffuse tail is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-diffuse-tail-definition}
 \tau_{\rm ac}(\dd r,\dd\theta)
 =p r^{-p-1}\log r\,\mathbf1_{\{r\ge R_0\}}
   \dd r\,\sigma(\dd\theta).
\end{equation}
Changing variables \(r=R\rho\) and applying dominated convergence against
each compactly supported continuous test function on
\(\RR^d\setminus\{0\}\) proves
\(R^p(\log R)^{-1}\tau_{\rm ac}(R\,\mathord\cdot)\to\mu_\sigma\).

For the clustered tail, define recursively
\[
 s_{n+1}=s_n+s_n^{-1/2},\qquad R_n=e^{s_n},\qquad
 W_n=\int_{s_n}^{s_{n+1}}pu e^{-pu}\dd u.
\]
Choose an antipodally symmetric equal-weight empirical probability
\(\pi_n\) on \(S^{d-1}\), supported on
\(N_n\asymp s_n^{1/4}\) points, so that \(\pi_n\Rightarrow\sigma\), and set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-clustered-tail-definition}
 \tau_{\rm cl}=\sum_{n\ge1}W_n(\theta\mapsto R_n\theta)_\#\pi_n.
\end{equation}
This tail has the same vague limit.  To verify the assertion without a
moving-set argument, let \(f\in C_c(\RR^d\setminus\{0\})\) and write
\(R=e^s\).  Then
\[
 \frac{e^{ps}}s\int f(x/e^s)\tau_{\rm cl}(\dd x)
 =\frac{e^{ps}}s\sum_nW_n
   \int f(e^{s_n-s}\theta)\pi_n(\dd\theta).
\]
Only indices with \(s_n-s\) in a fixed compact interval occur.  On this
interval the maximal mesh \(s_n^{-1/2}\) tends to zero, \(u/s\to1\)
uniformly, and \(\pi_n\Rightarrow\sigma\) uniformly over the indices in
question.  The right side is therefore a Riemann sum converging to
\[
 \int_\RR pe^{-pv}\int_{S^{d-1}}f(e^v\theta)
 \sigma(\dd\theta)\dd v
 =\int f\dd\mu_\sigma.
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:critical-vague-tail-common-limit} for the two tails.

Both tails have arbitrarily small total mass and covariance when \(s_1\) is

% sec_covariance_proxy_defect.tex -- exact covariance-proxy KL defect
\section{The Covariance-Matched Poisson Defect}
\label{sec:covariance-proxy-defect}

Finite covariance determines the quadratic mode but, in dimensions at least
four, does not force the matched entropy to attain the corresponding
coefficient.  The following result identifies the complete nonnegative
remainder without imposing a tail class or an absolute moment above order
two.

\begin{theorem}[Covariance-proxy defect decomposition]
\label{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
\label{thm:finite-covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with mean
\(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
 h_t=P_t^{(d)}*\nu,
 \qquad g_t=P_t^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma),
 \qquad H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t),
\]
and let \(b_\Sigma\) be the bounded covariance mode in
\eqref{eq:multidim-leading-theorem-density-mode}.  For all sufficiently
large \(t\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy}
 k_{\Sigma,t}(x)
 :=g_t(x)\left[1+t^{-2}b_\Sigma
 \left(\frac{x-\bar\gamma}{t}\right)\right]
\end{equation}
is a probability density.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 :=t^4D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t}).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
 t^4H_d(t)
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+\mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)+o(1)
 \qquad(t\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No density of \(\nu\), regular-variation hypothesis, angular balance, or
higher absolute moment is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
After translation, assume \(\bar\gamma=0\), and write
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^{(d+1)/2}.
\]
In normalized coordinates,
\[
 q_t(y):=\frac{h_t(ty)}{g_t(ty)}
 =\EE F_y(X/t),
 \qquad \delta_t=q_t-1,
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma.
\]
The covariance calculation in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} gives
\(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\).  Since \(b_\Sigma\) is bounded,
\(1+u_t\ge1/2\) for all sufficiently large \(t\), proving that
\eqref{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy} is positive and has integral one.

We first establish the only asymptotic input needed below:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}
 t^2\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)\delta_t(y)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 \longrightarrow
 \int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)^2\,\Omega_d(\dd y).
\end{equation}
Set
\[
 J(z)=\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)F_y(z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y.
\]
The function \(b_\Sigma\) is even, smooth, and has bounded second
derivatives.  Translation in the last integral shows that \(J\) is even,
\(J(0)=0\), and its Hessian is bounded on the unit ball.  Hence
\(|J(z)|\le C|z|^2\) for \(|z|\le1\).  For \(|z|\ge1\), positivity and
normalization of the translated Poisson density give
\(|J(z)|\le\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty\le
\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty|z|^2\).  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing}
 |J(z)|\le C_{d,\Sigma}|z|^2,
 \qquad z\in\RR^d.
\end{equation}

Let \(B_2(y,z)\) denote the homogeneous degree-two Taylor term of
\(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Taylor expansion under the integral gives
\[
 J(z)=\int b_\Sigma(y)B_2(y,z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)+o(|z|^2).
\]
The covariance-mode identity proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} is
\(\EE B_2(y,X)=b_\Sigma(y)\).  Since \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\),
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing} and dominated
convergence imply
\[
 \EE[t^2J(X/t)]
 \longrightarrow
 \int b_\Sigma(y)\EE B_2(y,X)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d.
\]
Because \(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\), the left side is precisely the
left side of \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}.

We also have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness}
 \|\delta_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, Minkowski's integral inequality bounds this norm by
\[
 \EE\left\|P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-X/t)-P_1^{(d)}
 \right\|_{L^1(\RR^d)},
\]
which tends to zero by translation continuity and dominated convergence.

Put \(r_t=\delta_t-u_t\).  Equations
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing}
 t^2\int b_\Sigma r_t\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \|r_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Uniformly in \(y\),
\[
 \log(1+u_t)=u_t+O(t^{-4}).
\]
It follows from \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder}
 \int r_t\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d=o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
On the other hand, with
\(\Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s\), boundedness of \(b_\Sigma\) gives
\begin{align}
 \int(1+u_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 &=\int\Phi(u_t)\dd\Omega_d \notag\\
 &=\frac{t^{-4}}2\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d+O(t^{-6})
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+O(t^{-6}).
 \label{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy}
\end{align}
Combining \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy} yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic}
 \int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}

Finally, the exact logarithmic identity is
\[
 H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t})
 +\int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d.
\]
 All terms are finite for large \(t\).  Indeed, convexity of relative entropy
 and
 \[
  D_{\rm KL}\!\left(P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-z)\,\middle\|\,
  P_1^{(d)}\right)
  \le \frac{d+1}{2}\log\{2(1+|z|^2)\}
  \le C_d(1+|z|^2)
 \]
 bound \(H_d(t)\) by an integrable average under
 \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\).  The first inequality follows from
 \(1+|w+z|^2\le2(1+|w|^2)(1+|z|^2)\).  Moreover,
 \(\log(1+u_t)\) is bounded.  Substitution of
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic} proves
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}.  Nonnegativity of relative
entropy then gives \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Raw-tail Poisson-energy decomposition]
\label{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
mean \(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Write
\(\nu_c\) for its centred law, put \(a=(d+1)/2\), and define
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^a,
 \qquad
 \Phi(v)=(1+v)\log(1+v)-v.
\]
For \(t>0\), let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential}
 V_t^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>t\}}F_y(x/t)\,\nu_c(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}
 H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, the covariance-proxy defect in
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 =t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(1),
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No regular-variation or absolute-continuity hypothesis is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Translation permits us to assume that \(\nu\) is centred.  Set
\[
 \delta_t(y)=\int\{F_y(x/t)-1\}\nu(\dd x),
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma,
 \qquad \tau_t=\nu(|x|>t).
\]
Finite second moment gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness}
 \tau_t\le t^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Let \(\ell_y(z)\) and \(B_2(y,z)\) be the homogeneous degree-one and
degree-two Taylor terms of \(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Direct differentiation of
the rational function \(F_y\), together with translation continuity of
the first two derivatives of \(P_1^{(d)}\) in \(L^1(\RR^d)\), gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_\infty
 +\|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}
 \le \varepsilon_d(|z|)|z|^2
\end{equation}
for \(|z|\le1\), where \(\varepsilon_d\) is bounded on \([0,1]\) and
\(\varepsilon_d(r)\to0\) as \(r\downarrow0\).  The Taylor coefficients
themselves satisfy, for \(j=1,2\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms}
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_\infty+
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\le C_d|z|^j,
 \qquad B_1=\ell.
\end{equation}

Centering and the covariance-mode identity
\(\int B_2(y,x)\nu(\dd x)=b_\Sigma(y)\) now yield the exact split
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-quotient-split}
 \delta_t=u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t+R_t,
\end{equation}
where
\[
 R_t=
 \int_{|x|\le t}\{F_\cdot(x/t)-1-\ell_\cdot(x/t)
                   -B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x)
 -\int_{|x|>t}\{\ell_\cdot(x/t)+B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x).
\]
Equations \eqref{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus} and
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms} imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-remainder-smallness}
 \|R_t\|_\infty+\|R_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Indeed, after multiplication by \(t^2\), the interior remainder is
dominated by \(C_d|x|^2\) and converges pointwise to zero.  The omitted
linear and quadratic tail terms are bounded respectively by
\[
 Ct^{-1}\int_{|x|>t}|x|\nu(\dd x)
 \le Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x).
\]

The identity
\(F_y(z)\Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-potential-normalization}
 V_t^\nu\ge0,
 \qquad \int V_t^\nu\dd\Omega_d=\tau_t.
\end{equation}
We next justify the nonlinear separation.  If \(|u|\le1/4\) and \(v\ge0\),
two integrations of \(\Phi''(s)=(1+s)^{-1}\) over the rectangle joining
\((0,0)\) to \((u,v)\) give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-phi-interaction}
 |\Phi(u+v)-\Phi(u)-\Phi(v)|\le C|u|v.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness} and

 succeeded in 291ms:
\label{thm:low-dimensional-finite-covariance-threshold}
Let \(1\le d\le3\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
finite mean.  With
\[
 H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}\!\left(P_t^{(d)}*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 P_t^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma)\right),
\]
one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:low-dimensional-covariance-iff}
 \EE|X|^2<\infty
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H_d(t)<\infty.
\end{equation}
If the covariance is finite, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:low-dimensional-covariance-limit}
 H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
If it is infinite, then \(t^4H_d(t)\to+\infty\) in the extended sense.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(d\le3\), \(q_d=2\).  Thus Theorem
\ref{thm:multidim-l2-moment-threshold} and the \(L^2\) alternative in
Theorem~\ref{thm:second-order-quotient-kl-coefficient} prove
\eqref{eq:low-dimensional-covariance-limit}.  The entropy representation is
valid in the extended sense by change of variables; for large \(t\), the
\(L^2\) convergence makes \(\Phi(\delta_t^{(d)})\) integrable because
\(\Phi(s)\le C s^2\) on \([-1,\infty)\).

Conversely, if \(\EE|X|^2=\infty\), some unit vector \(e\) satisfies
\(\EE(e\cdot X)^2=\infty\).  Projection onto \(e\) sends the radial
\(d\)-dimensional Poisson law to the one-dimensional Cauchy law at the same
scale.  The data-processing inequality and
Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-leading-kl-variance-threshold} therefore give
\[
 H_d(t)\ge
 D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu_e\|P_t(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma_e)),
\]
whose product with \(t^4\) tends to \(+\infty\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Finite covariance does not control high-dimensional KL]
\label{prop:high-dimensional-finite-covariance-obstruction}
For every \(d\ge4\), there exists a centred discrete probability law on
\(\RR^d\) with positive-definite finite covariance such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:high-dimensional-finite-covariance-obstruction}
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H_d(t)=+\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Choose positive \(\alpha_j\) with \(\sum_j\alpha_j<\infty\), radii
\(R_j\uparrow\infty\), and weights
\(w_j=\alpha_jR_j^{-2}\).  After rescaling the sequence if necessary,
\(W=\sum_jw_j<1\).  Begin with
\[
 (1-W)\delta_0+\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{w_j}{2}
 (\delta_{R_je_1}+\delta_{-R_je_1}),
\]
and transfer an arbitrarily small amount of the mass at zero to a bounded
centred symmetric law spanning \(\RR^d\).  The covariance is then positive
definite and finite because \(\sum_jw_jR_j^2=\sum_j\alpha_j<\infty\).

Fix a sufficiently small constant \(\kappa>0\), and set
\[
 t_j=\kappa R_jw_j^{1/(d+1)}.
\]
The radii may be chosen recursively so that \(t_j\to\infty\).  Let \(A_j\)
be the ball of radius \(t_j\) centred at \(R_je_1\).  The \(j\)th mixture
component gives
\[
 (P_{t_j}^{(d)}*\nu)(A_j)\ge c_dw_j,
\]
whereas, since \(t_j/R_j\to0\), the centred reference satisfies
\[
 P_{t_j}^{(d)}(A_j)
 \le C_d(t_j/R_j)^{d+1}
 =C_d\kappa^{d+1}w_j.
\]
Choose \(\kappa\) so that the second constant is strictly smaller than the
first.  Data processing to the partition \(\{A_j,A_j^c\}\) and
Lemma~\ref{lem:bernoulli-kl-linear-separation} then give
\(H_d(t_j)\ge c'_dw_j\).  Hence
\[
 t_j^4H_d(t_j)
 \ge c''_dR_j^4w_j^{1+4/(d+1)}
 =c''_d\alpha_j^{1+4/(d+1)}
 R_j^{2(d-3)/(d+1)}.
\]
The exponent of \(R_j\) is positive exactly when \(d>3\).  Choosing the
radii recursively makes the final expression tend to infinity.
\end{proof}

For the high-dimensional entropy topology, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-kl-exponents}
 q_d:=\frac{d+5}{d+1},
 \qquad
 p_d:=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5}.
\end{equation}
Thus \(1<q_d<2\), \(2<p_d<4\), and \(p_dq_d=4\) when \(d\ge4\).

\begin{lemma}[Critical translation remainder]
\label{lem:critical-lq-translation-remainder}
Let \(d\ge4\), let \(p_d,q_d\) be as in
\eqref{eq:critical-kl-exponents}, and put \(m=\lfloor p_d\rfloor\).
If \(T_m(y,z)\) is the Taylor polynomial of \(F_y(z)\) through degree
\(m\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-lq-exact-translation-norm}
 \|F_\cdot(z)\|_{L^{q_d}(\Omega_d)}^{q_d}
 =1+\frac{2(d+5)}{(d+1)(d+3)}|z|^2
 +\frac{3}{(d+1)(d+3)}|z|^4.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for a constant depending only on \(d\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-lq-global-remainder}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-T_m(\cdot,z)\|_{L^{q_d}(\Omega_d)}
 \le C_d
 \begin{cases}
 |z|^{m+1},& |z|\le1,\\
 |z|^{p_d},& |z|>1,
 \end{cases}
 \le C_d|z|^{p_d}.
\end{equation}
Here \(m=2\) for \(4\le d\le10\), while \(m=3\) for \(d\ge11\),
including the integer endpoint \(p_{11}=3\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since \(a(q_d-1)=2\), translation by \(z\) gives
\[
 \|F_\cdot(z)\|_{q_d}^{q_d}
 =c_d\int_{\RR^d}
 \frac{(1+|u+z|^2)^2}{(1+|u|^2)^{(d+5)/2}}\dd u.
\]
Write \(A=1+|u|^2\).  The odd term in the expansion of
\((A+|z|^2+2u\cdot z)^2\) integrates to zero, and rotational invariance
gives
\[
 \int(u\cdot z)^2(1+|u|^2)^{-(d+5)/2}\dd u
 =\frac{|z|^2}{d}\int |u|^2(1+|u|^2)^{-(d+5)/2}\dd u.
\]
The three normalized beta integrals needed here are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 c_d\int(1+|u|^2)^{-(d+1)/2}\dd u&=1,\\
 c_d\int(1+|u|^2)^{-(d+3)/2}\dd u&=\frac1{d+1},\\
 c_d\int(1+|u|^2)^{-(d+5)/2}\dd u
 &=\frac3{(d+1)(d+3)}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Substitution proves \eqref{eq:critical-lq-exact-translation-norm}.

For \(|z|\le1\), the local quotient--Taylor estimate
\eqref{eq:local-multidim-taylor-remainder} implies the first line of
\eqref{eq:critical-lq-global-remainder}.  For \(|z|>1\), the exact identity
gives \(\|F_\cdot(z)\|_{q_d}\le C_d|z|^{4/q_d}=C_d|z|^{p_d}\).
Every coefficient of \(T_m\) is bounded in \(y\), and \(m\le p_d\), so
\(\|T_m(\cdot,z)\|_{q_d}\le C_d|z|^{p_d}\).  Finally,
\(m+1>p_d\), which proves the last inequality for \(|z|\le1\).
\end{proof}

For \(0<\alpha<2\), let \(p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\) be the density of any
centred isotropic strictly \(\alpha\)-stable law on \(\RR^d\), and use the
spatial-scale normalization
\[
 p_s^{(\alpha,d)}(x)=s^{-d}p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(x/s),\qquad s>0.
\]
Changing the scale parameter in the characteristic exponent only changes
the choice of \(p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\), so this notation covers every isotropic
strictly stable kernel.  Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-critical-exponents}
 \beta=d+\alpha,\qquad
 q_{\alpha,d}:=\min\left\{2,1+\frac4\beta\right\},\qquad
 p_{\alpha,d}:=\frac4{q_{\alpha,d}}
 =\max\left\{2,\frac{4(d+\alpha)}{d+\alpha+4}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Write \(\Omega_{\alpha,d}(\dd y)=p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)\dd y\) and
\[
F_y^{(\alpha,d)}(z)
:=\frac{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y-z)}{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}.
\]

\begin{proposition}[Stable-kernel input package]
\label{prop:stable-kernel-input-package}
Put \(\beta=d+\alpha\).  For every multi-index \(\gamma\), there are positive
constants \(c,C,C_\gamma\), depending on \(d,\alpha\) and the chosen stable
scale, such that
\begin{align}
 c(1+|y|)^{-\beta}&\le p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)
 \le C(1+|y|)^{-\beta},\label{eq:stable-input-density}\\
 |\partial^\gamma p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)|
 &\le C_\gamma(1+|y|)^{-\beta-|\gamma|},\label{eq:stable-input-derivative}\\
 \frac{|\partial^\gamma p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)|}
 {p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}
 &\le C_\gamma(1+|y|)^{-|\gamma|}.
 \label{eq:stable-input-derivative-quotient}
\end{align}
In particular, \(p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\) is strictly positive and smooth,
\(\|\nabla\log p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\|_\infty<\infty\), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-input-translation-continuity}
 \|\partial^\gamma p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(\mathord\cdot-z)
       -\partial^\gamma p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\|_1\longrightarrow0
 \qquad(z\to0).
\end{equation}
If the characteristic exponent is changed from \(|\xi|^\alpha\) to
\(a|\xi|^\alpha\), put \(\lambda=a^{1/\alpha}\).  The new unit-scale density
is \(p_{1,a}(y)=\lambda^{-d}p_1(y/\lambda)\).  Thus the density bound has
factor \(\lambda^{-d}\), the derivative bound has factor
\(\lambda^{-d-|\gamma|}\), and the quotient bound has factor
\(\lambda^{-|\gamma|}\), with \(1+|y|\) replaced throughout by
\(1+|y|/\lambda\).  Equivalently, all later
constants are locally uniform when \(a\) ranges in a compact subset of
\((0,\infty)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Properties (G)(i)--(iii) of \cite{IshigeKawakamiMichihisa2017}, also recorded
in the refined formulation of
\cite{IshigeKawakamiMichihisa2022RefinedFractional}, give strict positivity,
smoothness, the derivative upper bounds, and the positive tail asymptotic
\(p_1(y)\asymp |y|^{-d-\alpha}\) outside a sufficiently large ball.  The
tail asymptotic supplies the lower bound in
\eqref{eq:stable-input-density} there.  On the remaining compact ball,
strict positivity and continuity give a positive minimum; enlarging the
upper constant completes the global two-sided density bound.  Dividing
\eqref{eq:stable-input-derivative} by this lower bound proves
\eqref{eq:stable-input-derivative-quotient}, including the log-gradient
bound.  The derivative estimate makes every \(\partial^\gamma p_1\)
integrable, so ordinary \(L^1\)-translation continuity gives
\eqref{eq:stable-input-translation-continuity}.  The assertions about \(a\)
follow directly by differentiating the displayed dilation formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Stable critical translation remainder]
\label{lem:stable-critical-translation-remainder}
Let \(d\ge1\), \(0<\alpha<2\), \(p=p_{\alpha,d}\),
\(q=q_{\alpha,d}\), and \(m=\lfloor p\rfloor\).  If
\(T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(y,z)\) is the Taylor polynomial of
\(F_y^{(\alpha,d)}(z)\) at \(z=0\) through degree \(m\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-translation-lq-growth}
 c_{\alpha,d,q}(1+|z|)^{\beta(q-1)}
 \le \|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(z)\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}^q
 \le C_{\alpha,d,q}(1+|z|)^{\beta(q-1)}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-critical-global-remainder}
 \|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(z)-T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(\cdot,z)\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}
 \le C_{\alpha,d}
 \begin{cases}
  |z|^{m+1},&|z|\le1,\\
  |z|^p,&|z|>1,
 \end{cases}
 \le C_{\alpha,d}|z|^p.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-kernel-input-package} supplies all density,
derivative, quotient, and translation estimates used below.  The upper
bound in \eqref{eq:stable-translation-lq-growth} follows from
\[

 succeeded in 289ms:
Integrating over the ball and summing against all probability weights of
\(\eta\) yields
\[
 (p_{s_j}^{(\alpha,d)}*\eta)(E_j)
 \le C_{\alpha,d,r}(s_j/R_j)^\beta
 =C_{\alpha,d,r}\kappa^\beta w_j,
\]
which is the second assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform Bernoulli relative-entropy separation]
\label{lem:bernoulli-kl-linear-separation}
Let \(0<b<a\).  If \(u\ge aw\) and \(v\le bw\) are Bernoulli parameters,
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bernoulli-kl-linear-separation}
 D_{\rm KL}(\operatorname{Ber}(u)\|\operatorname{Ber}(v))
 \ge a\left\{\log\frac ab-1+\frac ba\right\}w.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If \(v=0<u\), the relative entropy is infinite.  Otherwise \(u>v>0\), and
\[
 (1-u)\log\frac{1-u}{1-v}\ge-(u-v).
\]
Writing \(\theta=v/u\le b/a<1\) therefore gives
\[
 D_{\rm KL}(\operatorname{Ber}(u)\|\operatorname{Ber}(v))
 \ge u\{\log(1/\theta)-1+\theta\}
 \ge aw\left\{\log\frac ab-1+\frac ba\right\},
\]
because the braced function is decreasing on \((0,1)\).
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Gaussian benchmark and priority.}
Let \(\rho_t(x)=(2\pi t)^{-d/2}e^{-|x|^2/(2t)}\).  Under a finite
exponential square moment for each centred input, Chen and Niles-Weed
\cite[Theorem~2.5]{ChenNilesWeed2022SmoothedWasserstein} prove that moment
matching below order \(r=n+1\), followed by a nonzero difference at order
\(r\), implies, with
\(\Delta m_\gamma=m_\gamma(\mu)-m_\gamma(\eta)\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chen-niles-weed-gaussian-kl-benchmark}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^rD_{\rm KL}(\mu*\rho_t\|\eta*\rho_t)
 =\frac12\sum_{|\gamma|=r}\frac{|\Delta m_\gamma|^2}{\gamma!}.
\end{equation}
Putting \(s=\sqrt t\) gives the same \(s^{-2r}\) scaling as below.  For the
standard Gaussian \(g=\rho_1\), the identities
\((-1)^{|\gamma|}\partial^\gamma g=H_\gamma g\) and
\(\int H_\gamma H_\delta g=\gamma!\mathbf1_{\gamma=\delta}\) identify the
right side of \eqref{eq:chen-niles-weed-gaussian-kl-benchmark} exactly with
the quadratic functional \eqref{eq:all-order-stable-leading-functional} at
the Gaussian boundary \(\alpha=2\).  Accordingly, the first-unmatched
quadratic KL mechanism and its positivity are prior work in the Gaussian
case.  The theorem below contributes the heavy-tailed range \(0<\alpha<2\),
the critical complete-moment exponent, endpoint sufficiency, and uniform
sharpness over those moment classes.

\begin{theorem}[All-order first unmatched tensor under stable smoothing]
\label{thm:all-order-stable-first-unmatched-moment}
Let \(d\ge1\), \(0<\alpha<2\), and let \(r\ge1\) be an integer.  Define
\(q_{r,\alpha,d}\) and \(p_{r,\alpha,d}\) by
\eqref{eq:all-order-stable-critical-exponents}.  Let \(\nu,\eta\) be
probability laws on \(\RR^d\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-moment-hypotheses}
 m_\gamma(\nu)=m_\gamma(\eta)\quad(|\gamma|<r),
 \qquad
 \int|x|^{p_{r,\alpha,d}}(\nu+\eta)(\dd x)<\infty.
\end{equation}
Put
\[
 \Delta_r:=\int_{\RR^d}x^{\otimes r}(\nu-\eta)(\dd x)
 \in\operatorname{Sym}^r(\RR^d),
\]
so its multi-index coordinates are
\(\Delta m_\gamma=m_\gamma(\nu)-m_\gamma(\eta)\), and suppose
\(\Delta_r\ne0\).  With the Euclidean contraction of symmetric tensors,
define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-leading-mode}
 B_{\Delta_r}(y):=
 \frac{(-1)^r}{r!p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}
 \langle\Delta_r,D^rp_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)\rangle
 =\frac{(-1)^r}{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}
 \sum_{|\gamma|=r}\frac{\Delta m_\gamma}{\gamma!}
 \partial^\gamma p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-leading-functional}
 \mathcal Q_{\alpha,d,r}(\Delta_r)
 :=\frac12\int_{\RR^d}B_{\Delta_r}(y)^2
                  \Omega_{\alpha,d}(\dd y).
\end{equation}
Then \(\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d,r}(\Delta_r)>0\), the quadratic form is
invariant under \(\Delta_r\mapsto U^{\otimes r}\Delta_r\) for every
\(U\in O(d)\), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-first-unmatched-asymptotic}
 D_{\rm KL}(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\|p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\eta)
 =\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d,r}(\Delta_r)s^{-2r}+o(s^{-2r}).
\end{equation}

The exponent \(p_{r,\alpha,d}\) is optimal as a uniform sufficient
absolute-moment hypothesis, with its endpoint included.  More precisely,
if \(d+\alpha>2r\), then for every
\(r\le\rho<p_{r,\alpha,d}\) there are probability laws \(\nu,\eta\)
having finite \(\rho\)th absolute moments, matching every tensor moment
below order \(r\), and having a finite nonzero order-\(r\) tensor difference,
but
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-sharpness}
 \limsup_{s\to\infty}s^{2r}
 D_{\rm KL}(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\|p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\eta)=+\infty.
\end{equation}
For \(0<\rho<r\), finiteness of the \(\rho\)th absolute moment does not
even guarantee that the tensor in \eqref{eq:all-order-stable-leading-mode}
is defined throughout the moment class.  Thus the latter observation is a
domain obstruction, whereas \eqref{eq:all-order-stable-sharpness} is the
analytic sharpness statement on laws for which the leading tensor exists.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write \(q=q_{r,\alpha,d}\), \(p=p_{r,\alpha,d}\), and
\(m=\lfloor p\rfloor\).  Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:all-order-stable-critical-remainder} to both laws.  Their
common moments below order \(r\) cancel, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-two-quotient-split}
 q_s^\nu-q_s^\eta=v_s+e_s,
 \quad
 v_s=\sum_{j=r}^m s^{-j}(B_j^\nu-B_j^\eta),
 \quad
 e_s=r_s^\nu-r_s^\eta.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 s^rv_s\longrightarrow B_{\Delta_r}\quad\hbox{in }L^\infty,
 \qquad \|e_s\|_q=o(s^{-p}).
\]
The intermediate modes \(r<j\le m\) in
\eqref{eq:all-order-stable-two-quotient-split} are essential: unless their
moments also match, they are not part of the \(o(s^{-p})\) remainder.

No part of this argument requires \(r\ge2\).  At \(r=1\), the endpoint
formula still gives \(p\ge1\), hence the assumed \(p\)-moment supplies the
first moments and \(\EE|X|<\infty\); also \(m+1>p\) and \(pq=2\).  Thus the
critical remainder and the two-background transfer have exactly the powers
needed for an \(o(s^{-2})\) error.  Lemma~\ref{lem:stable-finite-difference-blocks}
records the corresponding first-difference block used for sharpness.

Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-kernel-input-package} implies
\(\|\nabla\log p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\|_\infty\le L_{\alpha,d}\).  Hence, if
\(X\) has law \(\lambda\),
\[
 q_s^\lambda(y)
 \ge\EE\exp(-L_{\alpha,d}|X|/s)
 \ge\exp(-L_{\alpha,d}\EE|X|/s),
\]
uniformly in \(y\).  The last expression tends to one.  Translation
continuity of \(p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\) in \(L^1\) gives, by Tonelli and dominated
convergence,
\[
 \|q_s^\eta-1\|_{L^1(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}
 \le\int\|p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(\mathord\cdot-x/s)
              -p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\|_1\,\eta(\dd x)\longrightarrow0;
\]
the integrand is bounded by two.  A second Tonelli calculation gives
\(\int q_s^\lambda\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}=1\) for every probability law
\(\lambda\).  These are precisely the positive lower bound, mass
normalization, and \(L^1\)-convergence required of the denominator by
Lemma~\ref{lem:two-background-critical-bregman-transfer}; no
\(L^\infty\)-convergence of \(q_s^\eta\) to one is used.  Taking
\(g_s=q_s^\eta\) in that lemma proves
\eqref{eq:all-order-stable-first-unmatched-asymptotic}.

Boundedness of \(B_{\Delta_r}\) makes
\(\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d,r}\) finite.  If it were zero, then
\[
 \sum_{|\gamma|=r}\frac{\Delta m_\gamma}{\gamma!}
 \partial^\gamma p_1^{(\alpha,d)}=0
\]
almost everywhere.  Fourier transformation in the sense of tempered
distributions gives a homogeneous polynomial in \(\xi\), with coefficients
\(\Delta m_\gamma/\gamma!\), multiplied by the nowhere-vanishing stable
characteristic function.  The polynomial must vanish identically, contrary
to \(\Delta_r\ne0\).  This proves strict positivity.  If \(U\in O(d)\),
isotropy and the tensor chain rule give
\(B_{U^{\otimes r}\Delta_r}(Uy)=B_{\Delta_r}(y)\).  Orthogonal invariance of
\(\Omega_{\alpha,d}\) then proves the asserted invariance of \(\mathcal Q\).
For \(d\ge2\), under the homogeneous-polynomial identification, the standard
trace/harmonic decomposition
\[
 \operatorname{Sym}^r(\RR^d)
 =\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}
   |x|^{2k}\mathcal H_{r-2k}
\]
is multiplicity-free under \(O(d)\).  Hence the self-adjoint operator of
\(\mathcal Q\) is scalar on each harmonic summand; the positivity just proved
shows that all these scalars are strictly positive.  No formula for the
individual scalars is needed below.

It remains to establish sharpness.  Assume \(\beta=d+\alpha>2r\), so
\[
 q=1+\frac{2r}{\beta},\qquad p=\frac{2r}{q}>r.
\]
Fix a unit vector \(e\), and let \(A_R^\pm\) be the probability blocks from
Lemma~\ref{lem:stable-finite-difference-blocks}.

Choose \(a_j>0\) with \(\sum_ja_j<\infty\).  Choose the radii recursively so
that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-separated-block-radii}
 R_{j+1}>4(r+1)R_j,
\end{equation}
and enlarge each new radius further whenever necessary below.  Put
\(w_j=a_jR_j^{-\rho}\), with \(\sum_jw_j<1\), and define
\[
 \nu=\left(1-\sum_jw_j\right)\delta_0+\sum_jw_jA_{R_j}^+,
 \qquad
 \eta=\left(1-\sum_jw_j\right)\delta_0+\sum_jw_jA_{R_j}^-.
\]
They have finite \(\rho\)th moments.  Since \(\rho\ge r\),
\eqref{eq:stable-block-moment-identities} also shows that their
order-\(r\) tensor difference is finite and nonzero.

The endpoint \(x_j=(r+1)R_je\) belongs to \(A_{R_j}^+\) and not to
\(A_{R_j}^-\).  Let \(E_j\) be the ball of radius
\[
 s_j=\kappa R_jw_j^{1/\beta}
\]
about \(x_j\).  Recursive enlargement of \(R_j\) makes \(s_j\to\infty\);
also \(s_j/R_j=\kappa w_j^{1/\beta}<1/4\) for all sufficiently large \(j\).
Lemma~\ref{lem:stable-moving-ball-probability-separation} gives both
probability bounds, after explicitly separating \(E_j\) from every
opposite-sign atom in the same, preceding, and succeeding blocks.
Choose \(\kappa\) so that
\(C_{\alpha,d,r}\kappa^\beta<a_r\).  Data processing to
\(\{E_j,E_j^c\}\), followed by
Lemma~\ref{lem:bernoulli-kl-linear-separation}, gives
\[
 D_{\rm KL}(p_{s_j}^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\|
            p_{s_j}^{(\alpha,d)}*\eta)\ge c w_j.
\]
Consequently,
\[
 s_j^{2r}D_{\rm KL}(p_{s_j}^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\|
                    p_{s_j}^{(\alpha,d)}*\eta)
 \ge cR_j^{2r}w_j^{1+2r/\beta}
 =ca_j^qR_j^{2r-\rho q}.
\]
The final exponent is positive exactly when \(\rho<p=2r/q\).
Recursive enlargement of \(R_j\) proves
\eqref{eq:all-order-stable-sharpness}.  Finally, for \(\rho<r\), a
one-dimensional Pareto law supported on the \(e\)-axis may have finite
\(\rho\)th and infinite \(r\)th absolute moment, proving the stated domain
obstruction.
\end{proof}

\[

\]
Let \(G_n\nu\) and \(\pi_n\) be its \(n\)-node Gaussian quadrature law and

strictly \(\alpha\)-stable kernel.  If
\[
 \EE|X_c|^{p_{\alpha,d}}<\infty,
 \qquad
 p_{\alpha,d}=\max\left\{2,

 D_{\rm KL}\!\left(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\,\middle\|\,

 =\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}+o(s^{-4}),
\]
with \(\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}\) defined in

as a uniform sufficient finite-moment hypothesis, including its endpoint;
no smaller absolute-moment exponent works uniformly over its full moment
class.

More generally, let \(r\ge1\), let \(\nu,\eta\) be probability laws on
\(\RR^d\), and assume that their tensor moments agree below order \(r\),
their order-\(r\) tensor difference \(\Delta_r\) is nonzero, and both have
finite \(p_{r,\alpha,d}\)th absolute moments, where
\[
 q_{r,\alpha,d}=\min\left\{2,1+\frac{2r}{d+\alpha}\right\},
 \qquad
 p_{r,\alpha,d}=\frac{2r}{q_{r,\alpha,d}}.
\]
Then
\[
 D_{\rm KL}(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\|p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\eta)
 =\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d,r}(\Delta_r)s^{-2r}+o(s^{-2r}),
\]
where \(\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d,r}\) is the strictly positive quadratic
functional in \eqref{eq:all-order-stable-leading-functional}.  The endpoint
\(p_{r,\alpha,d}\) is optimal as a uniform sufficient absolute-moment
exponent.  This extends the Gaussian theorem of Chen--Niles-Weed from
sub-Gaussian inputs to heavy-tailed stable kernels with the critical complete
moment assumption.  For \(d+\alpha>2r\), sharpness below the endpoint holds even
among pairs with finite nonzero order-\(r\) tensor difference; below \(r\)
the obstruction is instead that the coefficient need not be defined.  In
dimension one, Corollary~\ref{cor:stable-gaussian-quadrature-threshold}
converts this statement into a finite/infinite \(2n\)th-moment equivalence
for Gaussian quadrature under every symmetric stable index.

For an individual centred law with finite covariance \(\Sigma\), define
\[
 V_s^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>s\}}
 \frac{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y-x/s)}
      {p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}\,\nu(\dd x).
\]
Then the exact law-by-law decomposition is
\[
 H_{\alpha,d}(s)=\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_s^\nu(y))\,\Omega_{\alpha,d}(\dd y)
 +o(s^{-4}),
\]
and hence
\[
 s^4H_{\alpha,d}(s)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 s^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_s^\nu)\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}\longrightarrow0.
\]
This clause assumes no regular variation, absolute continuity, or moment
above order two.
\end{maintheorem}

\begin{maintheorem}{Domain and endpoint closure for Cauchy relative entropy}
\label{thm:main-C}
Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on \(\RR\) with mean \(\bar{\gamma}\) and
finite centred second moment.  Put
\[
\begin{aligned}
h_t&=P_t*\nu,\\
g_t&=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\\

 succeeded in 297ms:
whenever \(|u|\le1/4\) and \(u+v\ge-1\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The lower bound is convexity.  If \(|v|\le1/2\), the segment between
\(u\) and \(u+v\) is contained in \([-3/4,3/4]\).  Since
\(\Phi''(s)=(1+s)^{-1}\) there, Taylor's theorem gives an upper bound
\(C v^2\le C|v|^q\).  If \(v<-1/2\), the admissible pairs
\((u,v)\) range over a compact set and \(|v|^q\ge2^{-q}\); continuity at
\(u+v=-1\) gives the result.  If \(v>1/2\), direct expansion of the left
side shows that it is at most \(C(1+v)\log(1+v)+Cv\), which is bounded by
\(C_qv^q\).  These three regions prove
\eqref{eq:critical-bregman-pointwise-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Optimal uniform sufficient moment exponent for stable KL asymptotics]
\label{thm:high-dimensional-kl-moment-threshold}
Let \(d\ge1\), \(0<\alpha<2\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on
\(\RR^d\) with mean \(\bar\gamma\).  Put
\(X=\gamma-\bar\gamma\), and assume
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-kl-moment-hypothesis}
 \EE|X|^{p_{\alpha,d}}<\infty,
 \qquad
 p_{\alpha,d}=\max\left\{2,
 \frac{4(d+\alpha)}{d+\alpha+4}\right\},
\end{equation}
then the covariance \(\Sigma=\EE[XX^{\mathsf T}]\) is finite and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-kl-leading-asymptotic}
 H_{\alpha,d}(s):=
 D_{\rm KL}\!\left(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 p_s^{(\alpha,d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma)\right)
 =\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}+o(s^{-4}),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-leading-covariance-functional}
 b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}(y)
 :=\frac1{2p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}
   \sum_{i,j=1}^d\Sigma_{ij}\partial_{ij}p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y),
 \qquad
 \mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)
 :=\frac12\int b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}^2\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}.
\end{equation}
The exponent is optimal only in the following uniform sense.  For every
\(0<r<2\), there is a mean-zero law with finite \(r\)th absolute moment
and \(\limsup_{s\to\infty}s^4H_{\alpha,d}(s)=+\infty\).  If
\(d+\alpha>4\), then for every \(2\le r<p_{\alpha,d}\) the counterexample
may additionally be chosen with positive-definite finite covariance.
Thus no smaller moment exponent implies
\eqref{eq:critical-kl-leading-asymptotic} uniformly over its entire moment
class, and the endpoint belongs to the sufficient side.

This is not a law-by-law moment criterion.  When \(d+\alpha>4\), finite
\(p_{\alpha,d}\)-moment is not asserted to be necessary for an individual
law; the exact finite-covariance criterion is the nonlinear raw-tail energy
in Theorem~\ref{thm:stable-law-by-law-decomposition} below.  When
\(d+\alpha\le4\), the exponent is two and boundedness of
\(s^4H_{\alpha,d}(s)\) does force finite covariance, as the proof below
shows.  For \(\alpha=1\) with the standard normalization
\(p_1^{(1,d)}=P_1^{(d)}\), one has
\(\mathcal Q_{1,d}=\mathcal Q_d\), so the theorem contains the radial
Poisson result above.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Translation permits us to assume \(\bar\gamma=0\).  Fix
\(q=q_{\alpha,d}\), \(p=p_{\alpha,d}\), and \(m=\lfloor p\rfloor\),
and write
\[
 R_m(y,z)=F_y^{(\alpha,d)}(z)-T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(y,z),
 \qquad r_s(y)=\EE R_m(y,X/s).
\]
Minkowski's inequality and
\eqref{eq:stable-critical-global-remainder} give
\[
 s^p\|R_m(\cdot,X/s)\|_q\le C_{\alpha,d}|X|^p.
\]
For each fixed \(X\), the local part of the same estimate and \(m+1>p\)
give
\[
 s^p\|R_m(\cdot,X/s)\|_q
 \le C_{\alpha,d}|X|^{m+1}s^{p-m-1}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Dominated convergence therefore yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-lq-integrated-remainder}
 \|r_s\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}=o(s^{-p}).
\end{equation}

Centering removes the linear Taylor layer.  Averaging the remaining layers
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-kl-quotient-decomposition}
 \delta_s^{(\alpha,d)}=u_s+r_s,
 \qquad
 u_s=s^{-2}b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}
       +\mathbf 1_{\{m=3\}}s^{-3}B_3,
\end{equation}
where \(B_3\) is a bounded linear combination of third-order modes.  In the
second case the required third moments exist because \(p\ge3\).  Hence
\(\|u_s\|_\infty=O(s^{-2})\).  The boundedness of all these modes follows
from Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-kernel-input-package}.


 succeeded in 296ms:
over moment classes is not a law-by-law necessity statement when
\(d+\alpha>4\).  The Gaussian boundary of the first-unmatched mechanism is
prior work.  Chen and Niles-Weed
\cite[Theorem~2.5]{ChenNilesWeed2022SmoothedWasserstein} write
\[
 \rho_t(x)=(2\pi t)^{-d/2}\exp(-|x|^2/(2t))
\]
and prove, under
\(\EE e^{\delta|X-\EE X|^2}+\EE e^{\delta|Y-\EE Y|^2}<\infty\), that if
the two laws first differ at order \(r=n+1\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-chen-niles-weed-kl}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^r
 D_{\rm KL}(\mu*\rho_t\|\eta*\rho_t)
 =\frac12\sum_{|\gamma|=r}
   \frac{|m_\gamma(\mu)-m_\gamma(\eta)|^2}{\gamma!}.
\end{equation}
With the spatial scale \(s=\sqrt t\), this is an \(s^{-2r}\) law.  If
\(g=\rho_1\), Gaussian Hermite orthogonality gives
\[
 \frac12\int\left\{
  \frac{(-1)^r}{g}\sum_{|\gamma|=r}
  \frac{\Delta m_\gamma}{\gamma!}\partial^\gamma g
 \right\}^{\!2}g\,\dd y
 =\frac12\sum_{|\gamma|=r}\frac{|\Delta m_\gamma|^2}{\gamma!}.
\]
Thus the positive quadratic tensor coefficient at an arbitrary first
unmatched order is not claimed here as a new mechanism.

Our increment is the heavy-tailed extension.  For \(0<\alpha<2\), the
Gaussian/Hermite and exponential-tail argument is replaced by a critical
stable translate-quotient estimate.  This determines the weakest uniform
complete-moment hypothesis, proves its endpoint with a little-\(o\)
remainder, and gives analytic counterexamples below it in the non-Hilbert
regime.  The mechanism holds at every first unmatched tensor order
\(r\ge1\).  With
\[
 q_{r,\alpha,d}=\min\left\{2,1+\frac{2r}{d+\alpha}\right\},
 \qquad
 p_{r,\alpha,d}=\frac{2r}{q_{r,\alpha,d}},
\]
Theorem~\ref{thm:all-order-stable-first-unmatched-moment} gives the
\(s^{-2r}\) quadratic tensor coefficient for two arbitrary inputs matching
all lower moments, under the endpoint \(p_{r,\alpha,d}\)-moment, and proves
that no smaller absolute-moment exponent works uniformly over the complete
moment class: for \(d+\alpha>2r\) this is analytic uniform sharpness on
pairs with a finite nonzero order-\(r\) tensor, whereas for
\(d+\alpha\le2r\) the value \(p_{r,\alpha,d}=r\) is only coefficient-domain
minimality below order \(r\).  The exact finite-covariance interface is instead supplied
by Theorem~\ref{thm:stable-law-by-law-decomposition}.  For a centred law put
\[
 V_s^\nu(y)=\int_{\{|x|>s\}}
 \frac{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y-x/s)}{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}\,\nu(\dd x).
\]
Then, without regular variation, absolute continuity, or a moment above
order two,
\[
 H_{\alpha,d}(s)=\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}
 +\int\Phi(V_s^\nu)\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}+o(s^{-4}).
\]
Thus the covariance coefficient is attained exactly when the nonlinear raw
tail energy is \(o(s^{-4})\).  When \(d+\alpha\le4\), the uniform exponent
is two and this tail condition follows from finite covariance; when
\(d+\alpha>4\), it is the additional law-by-law condition not expressible by
the uniform moment exponent alone.  The infinite
critical-moment boundary is genuinely different: Theorem
\ref{thm:critical-vague-tail-nonidentifiability} constructs two laws with the
same smooth multivariate regularly varying tail data and the same covariance,
one attaining the covariance coefficient and the other having unbounded
scaled entropy along a subsequence.
Theorem~\ref{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition} gives an
asymptotically exact
finite-covariance characterization of this distinction.  If
\(k_{\Sigma,t}\) denotes the positive Poisson density obtained by adjoining
the normalized quadratic mode \(t^{-2}b_\Sigma\) to the matched reference,
then
\[
 t^4H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 +t^4D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t})+o(1).
\]
Thus attainment of the covariance coefficient is equivalent to the
vanishing of the complete nonlinear proxy defect.  This statement requires
only finite covariance and does not impose a density or tail regularity on
the original law.
The leading multidimensional covariance formula is deliberately read through
this analytic route.  Finite covariance alone determines the bounded quadratic
mode \(b_\Sigma\) and the algebraic number \(\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)\), but it
does not by itself make \(\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)\) the coefficient of
\eqref{eq:intro-main-functional}.  It becomes the leading KL coefficient only
after a quotient/existence bridge is in force, for example the
\((\mathrm{MPJ}_{d,L})+(\mathrm{EX}_{d,L})\) package supplied here by
finite \(L\)-moment with \(L\ge d+1\), or the separate second-order quotient
criterion stated in Section~\ref{sec:entropy-asymptotics}.

The nearest prior work for the heavy-tailed density estimates is
Ishige--Kawakami--Michihisa
\cite{IshigeKawakamiMichihisa2017} and the refined follow-up
\cite{IshigeKawakamiMichihisa2022RefinedFractional}.  For every
\(0<\alpha<2\) and weighted moment order \(K\ge0\), those works subtract
moment-weighted derivatives of the stable heat kernel and prove scaled
\(L^q\) and weighted \(L^1\) remainder decay for the resulting density
expansion, including inhomogeneous and nonlinear refinements.  They do not
control the matched density quotient in
\(L^{q_{\alpha,d}}(p_1^{(\alpha,d)}\dd y)\) or transfer that critical
estimate through relative entropy; those are the steps that produce
\(p_{\alpha,d}\) here.  They also do not retain the unexpanded translate
mixture beyond the smoothing scale as the nonlinear entropy energy in
Theorem~\ref{thm:stable-law-by-law-decomposition}.
In contrast, Chen--Niles-Weed give the direct Gaussian KL precedent
\eqref{eq:intro-chen-niles-weed-kl}; the present comparison with the
fractional-diffusion literature is only a comparison of the heavy-tailed
analytic inputs and does not erase that priority.

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The full domain-closure motivation and comparison with the general
two-solution Bregman calculus are in \path{sec_introduction.tex}, the
paragraphs preceding ``Theorems,'' collected by
\path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.
\else
The third theorem is a domain-and-endpoint result for the standard
simultaneous-semigroup relative-entropy calculus.  For a symmetric jump
generator
\[
(L_Jf)(x)=\int\{f(y)-f(x)\}J(x,y)\,\dd y,
\qquad J(x,y)=J(y,x),
\]
and sufficiently regular positive solutions
\(\partial_t h_t=L_Jh_t\), \(\partial_t g_t=L_Jg_t\), set
\(u_t=h_t/g_t\).  The general generator identity is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-general-two-solution-generator-identity}
-\frac{\dd}{\dd t}D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t)

 succeeded in 305ms:
For the converse, define
\[
 R_n(u):=(-1)^n\left\{\cos u-
       \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(-1)^ku^{2k}}{(2k)!}\right\}.
\]
One has \(R_1=1-\cos\ge0\), while
\(R_n''=R_{n-1}\) and \(R_n(0)=R_n'(0)=0\).  Induction therefore gives
\(R_n\ge0\) on \(\RR\).  Moreover,
\(s^{2n}R_n(x/s)\to x^{2n}/(2n)!\).  If the \(2n\)th moment of \(\nu\) is
infinite, Fatou's lemma gives
\[
 s^{2n}\int R_n(x/s)\nu(\dd x)\longrightarrow+\infty.
\]
The corresponding integral for the finite-support law \(G_n\nu\) remains
bounded.  Since all even moments below \(2n\) agree, it follows that
\[
 s^{2n}\left|\Re\{\varphi_\nu(1/s)
                 -\varphi_{G_n\nu}(1/s)\}\right|\longrightarrow+\infty.
\]
At frequency \(1/s\), stable smoothing multiplies this difference by the
fixed nonzero number \(\widehat p_1^{(\alpha,1)}(1)\).  The dual
characterization of total variation and Pinsker's inequality now imply
\eqref{eq:stable-gaussian-quadrature-infinite}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The law-by-law finite-covariance decomposition}

The preceding theorem optimizes over moment classes.  The next theorem gives
the exact law-by-law interface under finite covariance alone.  It separates
the universal quadratic covariance energy from the full nonlinear energy of
the raw tail beyond the smoothing scale.

\begin{theorem}[Stable law-by-law tail decomposition]
\label{thm:stable-law-by-law-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), \(0<\alpha<2\), and let \(\nu\) be a centred probability law
on \(\RR^d\) with finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
 H_{\alpha,d}(s):=
 D_{\rm KL}\!\left(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 p_s^{(\alpha,d)}\right)
\]
and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-raw-tail-potential}
 V_s^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>s\}}
 \frac{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y-x/s)}
      {p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}\,\nu(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-law-by-law-decomposition}
 H_{\alpha,d}(s)=\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_s^\nu(y))\,\Omega_{\alpha,d}(\dd y)
 +o(s^{-4}).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-law-by-law-criterion}
 s^4H_{\alpha,d}(s)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 s^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_s^\nu)\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No regular-variation, absolute-continuity, or moment hypothesis above order two
is imposed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For brevity write
\[
 p_1=p_1^{(\alpha,d)},\qquad
 F_y(z)=F_y^{(\alpha,d)}(z)
 =\frac{p_1(y-z)}{p_1(y)}.
\]
Let \(\ell_y(z)\) and \(B_2(y,z)\) denote the homogeneous first- and
second-order Taylor terms of \(F_y(z)\) at zero.  The estimates in
Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-kernel-input-package} and Taylor's formula give, for
\(|z|\le1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-local-taylor-modulus}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_\infty
 +\|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}
 \le\varepsilon_{\alpha,d}(|z|)|z|^2,
\end{equation}
where \(\varepsilon_{\alpha,d}\) is bounded on \([0,1]\) and tends to zero
at zero.  The uniform term is in fact \(O(|z|^3)\).  For the \(L^1\)
term, multiplication by \(p_1(y)\) turns it into the second-order
translation remainder of \(p_1\).  Since \(D^2p_1\in L^1\), the integral
form of Taylor's theorem and translation continuity in \(L^1\) give
\[
 \int_0^1(1-\theta)|z|^2
 \|D^2p_1(\mathord\cdot-\theta z)-D^2p_1\|_1\dd\theta=o(|z|^2).
\]
The Taylor coefficients also satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-taylor-coefficient-norms}
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_\infty
 +\|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}
 \le C|z|^j,\qquad j=1,2,\quad B_1=\ell.
\end{equation}

Write
\[
 \delta_s(y)=\int\{F_y(x/s)-1\}\nu(\dd x),\qquad
 u_s=s^{-2}b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma},\qquad
 \tau_s=\nu(|x|>s).
\]
Finite covariance implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-tail-mass-smallness}
 \tau_s\le s^{-2}\int_{|x|>s}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)=o(s^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Centering and the identity
\(\int B_2(y,x)\nu(\dd x)=b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}(y)\) give the exact split
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-raw-tail-split}
 \delta_s=u_s+V_s^\nu-\tau_s+R_s,
\end{equation}
where
\[
 R_s=\int_{|x|\le s}\!
 \{F_\cdot(x/s)-1-\ell_\cdot(x/s)-B_2(\cdot,x/s)\}\nu(\dd x)
 -\int_{|x|>s}\!\{\ell_\cdot(x/s)+B_2(\cdot,x/s)\}\nu(\dd x).
\]
Equations \eqref{eq:stable-local-taylor-modulus} and
\eqref{eq:stable-taylor-coefficient-norms}, followed by dominated
convergence after multiplication by \(s^2\), show that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-raw-tail-remainder-smallness}
 \|R_s\|_\infty+\|R_s\|_{L^1(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}=o(s^{-2}).
\end{equation}
For the omitted tail modes one uses
\[
 s^{-1}\int_{|x|>s}|x|\nu(\dd x)
 \le s^{-2}\int_{|x|>s}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)
\]
and the analogous quadratic bound.

The translation identity for \(F_y\) gives the exact normalization
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-tail-potential-normalization}
 V_s^\nu\ge0,\qquad
 \int V_s^\nu\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}=\tau_s.
\end{equation}
All entropy integrals used below are finite.  Indeed,
Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-kernel-input-package} and
\(1+|w+z|\le(1+|w|)(1+|z|)\) give
\[
 D_{\rm KL}\!\left(p_1(\mathord\cdot-z)\,\middle\|\,p_1\right)
 \le C_{\alpha,d}+(d+\alpha)\log(1+|z|).
\]
Convexity of relative entropy and \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\) therefore make
\(H_{\alpha,d}(s)\) finite.  If \(\tau_s>0\), write
\(V_s^\nu=\tau_s q_s\), where \(q_s\) is the quotient of the normalized
tail mixture by \(p_1\).  Convexity gives
\[
 \int q_s\log q_s\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}
 \le \frac1{\tau_s}\int_{|x|>s}
 \left\{C_{\alpha,d}+(d+\alpha)\log(1+|x|/s)\right\}\nu(\dd x)<\infty.
\]
Since \(\Phi(\tau q)\le\tau q\log(1+q)\) for \(0\le\tau\le1\), and the
negative part of \(q\log q\) is bounded by \(e^{-1}\), this also proves
\(\int\Phi(V_s^\nu)\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}<\infty\).  The case \(\tau_s=0\)
is immediate.

For \(|u|\le1/4\) and \(v\ge0\), two integrations of
\(\Phi''(w)=(1+w)^{-1}\) yield
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-phi-interaction}
 |\Phi(u+v)-\Phi(u)-\Phi(v)|\le C|u|v.
\end{equation}
All negative terms in \eqref{eq:stable-raw-tail-split} are uniformly larger
than \(-1/2\) for large \(s\).  The mean-value theorem,
\(\log(1+v)\le v\), and
\eqref{eq:stable-tail-potential-normalization} therefore give
\begin{align}
 \int|\Phi(\delta_s)-\Phi(u_s+V_s^\nu-\tau_s)|
       \dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}&=o(s^{-4}),\label{eq:stable-remove-remainder}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_s+V_s^\nu-\tau_s)-\Phi(u_s+V_s^\nu)|
       \dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}&=o(s^{-4}),\label{eq:stable-remove-mass}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_s+V_s^\nu)-\Phi(u_s)-\Phi(V_s^\nu)|
       \dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}&=o(s^{-4}).\label{eq:stable-remove-interaction}
\end{align}
Indeed, the three left sides are bounded, respectively, by
\[
 C\|R_s\|_\infty\tau_s+Cs^{-2}\|R_s\|_1
 +C\|R_s\|_\infty\|R_s\|_1,
 \quad C\tau_s(\tau_s+s^{-2}),
 \quad C\|u_s\|_\infty\tau_s,
\]
and each is \(o(s^{-4})\).

Finally, integration of the second derivatives in
\eqref{eq:stable-leading-covariance-functional} gives
\(\int b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}=0\).  Boundedness of the
covariance mode then implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-covariance-phi-expansion}
 \int\Phi(u_s)\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}
 =\frac{s^{-4}}2\int b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}^2\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}
 +O(s^{-6})
 =\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}+O(s^{-6}).
\end{equation}
Since \(H_{\alpha,d}(s)=\int\Phi(\delta_s)\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}\),
equations \eqref{eq:stable-remove-remainder}--
\eqref{eq:stable-covariance-phi-expansion} prove
\eqref{eq:stable-law-by-law-decomposition}.  The equivalence
\eqref{eq:stable-law-by-law-criterion} follows because the tail energy is
nonnegative.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the decomposition resolves]
When \(d+\alpha\le4\), one has \(p_{\alpha,d}=2\), so every
finite-covariance law makes the tail energy \(o(s^{-4})\) by
Theorem~\ref{thm:high-dimensional-kl-moment-threshold}.  When
\(d+\alpha>4\), finite covariance alone need not do so.  In that regime
\eqref{eq:stable-law-by-law-criterion} is the exact missing law-by-law
condition: it neither replaces the nonlinear tail energy by a moment nor
assumes regular variation.
\end{remark}

assumes regular variation.
\end{remark}

\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-node-fourth-moment-coefficient}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^8D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_2\nu)
 =\frac5{64}\kappa^2.
\end{equation}
If \(\EE X^4=\infty\), the left side of
\eqref{eq:two-node-fourth-moment-coefficient} tends to \(+\infty\).  For a

norm is the identity proved in \path{sec_appendix.tex}, Lemma ``Normalized
fourth-moment defect algebra.''  Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:gauss-poisson-kl-threshold} and use
\(4^{-4}\binom63=5/64\).  In the symmetric case, exactness through degree
three and unit variance force the nodes and weights to be
\(\{-1,1\}\) and \(\{1/2,1/2\}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Square boundary at a missing Gaussian moment]
\label{thm:gauss-regular-variation-square-law}
Let \(n\ge2\), put \(p=2n\), and let \(\nu\) satisfy the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:gauss-poisson-kl-threshold}.  Assume, for an eventually
positive slowly varying function \(L\) and constants \(c_+,c_-\ge0\) with
\(c_++c_->0\),
\[
 \mathbb P(X>x)\sim c_+x^{-p}L(x),\qquad
 \mathbb P(X<-x)\sim c_-x^{-p}L(x),
\]
and assume
\[
 \ell_L(t)=\int_{x_0}^t\frac{L(s)}s\dd s\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Then, with
\(M_p(t)=\EE[|X|^p\mathbf1_{\{|X|\le t\}}]\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss-regular-variation-square-law}
 D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_n\nu)
 \sim C_p t^{-2p}M_p(t)^2,
 \qquad C_p=4^{-p}\binom{2p-2}{p-1}.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 M_p(t)\sim p(c_++c_-)\ell_L(t),
\]
so the boundary normalization is the square of the integrated slowly
varying factor and the leading constant is positive.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The quotient estimate proved for
Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}, before the
entropy transfer is applied, gives
\[
 q_t^\nu
 =\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}m_j(\nu)u_jt^{-j}
 +u_pt^{-p}M_p(t)+e_t,
 \qquad
 \|e_t\|_\infty+\|e_t\|_{L^1(\omega)}
 =o(t^{-p}M_p(t)).
\]
The finite-support law \(G_n\nu\) has an ordinary expansion through order
\(p\), and its moments through order \(p-1\) agree with those of \(\nu\).
Since \(M_p(t)\to\infty\), subtraction yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss-boundary-quotient-difference}
 q_t^\nu-q_t^{G_n\nu}
 =u_pt^{-p}M_p(t)+o_{L^\infty\cap L^1(\omega)}
 (t^{-p}M_p(t)).
\end{equation}
Interpolation also gives the same relative error in \(L^2(\omega)\).
Furthermore \(q_t^{G_n\nu}\to1\) uniformly.  Applying the Bregman expansion
from the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:moment-matched-poisson-kl} to
\eqref{eq:gauss-boundary-quotient-difference} gives
\[
 D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_n\nu)
 =\frac12t^{-2p}M_p(t)^2\int u_p^2\dd\omega
 +o(t^{-2p}M_p(t)^2).
\]
The Parseval calculation in that theorem identifies the constant as \(C_p\).
The asymptotic for \(M_p\) is Karamata's boundary integral theorem, exactly
as in Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The multidimensional \texorpdfstring{\(L^2\)}{L2} and entropy
thresholds}

Write \(a=(d+1)/2\) and
\[
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^a.
\]
Let \(T_2(y,z)\) denote the Taylor polynomial of \(F_y(z)\) at \(z=0\)
through homogeneous degree two.

\begin{lemma}[Large translations in the Cauchy quotient space]
\label{lem:multidim-large-translation-l2-growth}
For every \(d\ge1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:translated-quotient-l2-asymptotic}
 \|F_\cdot(z)\|_{L^2(\Omega_d)}^2
 \sim2^{-d}|z|^{d+1}\qquad(|z|\to\infty).
\end{equation}
If
\[
 q_d:=\max\left\{2,\frac{d+1}{2}\right\},
\]
then a constant \(C_d\) exists such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:second-order-l2-global-remainder}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-T_2(\cdot,z)\|_{L^2(\Omega_d)}
 \le C_d
 \begin{cases}|z|^3,&|z|\le1,\\ |z|^{q_d},&|z|>1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
With \(c_d=\Gamma((d+1)/2)/\pi^{(d+1)/2}\) and \(y=z+u\),
\[
 \|F_\cdot(z)\|_2^2
 =c_d\int_{\RR^d}
 \frac{(1+|z+u|^2)^a}{(1+|u|^2)^{d+1}}\dd u.
\]
After division by \(|z|^{d+1}\), dominated convergence applies and gives
\[
 c_d\int_{\RR^d}(1+|u|^2)^{-(d+1)}\dd u
 =\frac{\Gamma((d+1)/2)\Gamma(d/2+1)}
 {\sqrt\pi\Gamma(d+1)}=2^{-d}.
\]
For \(|z|\le1\), differentiation under the \(L^2(\Omega_d)\) norm through
third order is uniform on the unit ball, and Taylor's formula gives the
first line of \eqref{eq:second-order-l2-global-remainder}.  For \(|z|>1\),
\eqref{eq:translated-quotient-l2-asymptotic}, enlarged to a global upper
bound, and the fact that \(T_2\) is a degree-two polynomial in \(z\) with
\(L^2(\Omega_d)\) coefficients give
\[
 \|F_\cdot(z)-T_2(\cdot,z)\|_2
 \le C_d(|z|^{(d+1)/2}+|z|^2),
\]
which is the second line.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp absolute-moment condition for the \(L^2\) quotient]
\label{thm:multidim-l2-moment-threshold}
Let \(\nu\) be a centred probability law on \(\RR^d\), with covariance
matrix \(\Sigma\).  If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multidim-qd-moment}
 \EE|X|^{q_d}<\infty,
 \qquad q_d=\max\left\{2,\frac{d+1}{2}\right\},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multidim-l2-second-order-convergence}
 t^2\delta_t^{(d)}\longrightarrow b_\Sigma
 \quad\text{in }L^2(\Omega_d).
\end{equation}
For \(d\ge4\), this exponent is sharp among hypotheses stated solely as a
finite absolute moment: for every \(2\le p<q_d\), there is a centred law
with positive-definite covariance and finite \(p\)th absolute moment for
which \eqref{eq:multidim-l2-second-order-convergence} fails.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
After centering removes the degree-one term and covariance averages the
degree-two term, Minkowski's inequality gives
\[
 \|t^2\delta_t^{(d)}-b_\Sigma\|_2
 \le\EE\left[t^2
 \|F_\cdot(X/t)-T_2(\cdot,X/t)\|_2\right].
\]
The integrand converges pointwise to zero.  On \(|X|\le t\), the first line
of \eqref{eq:second-order-l2-global-remainder} bounds it by \(C|X|^2\);
on \(|X|>t\), the second line bounds it by
\(C|X|^{q_d}t^{2-q_d}\le C|X|^{q_d}\).  Dominated convergence proves
\eqref{eq:multidim-l2-second-order-convergence}.

For sharpness, let \(a=(d+1)/2>2\), choose \(p<a\), and take a symmetric
spike law with masses \(w_j/2\) at \(\pm R_je_1\), where
\(w_j=\alpha_jR_j^{-p}\), \(\sum_j\alpha_j<\infty\), and \(R_j\) grows
recursively.  Add a sufficiently small bounded symmetric component spanning
\(\RR^d\).  The resulting law has positive-definite finite covariance and
finite \(p\)th moment.  Choose
\[

 succeeded in 258ms:
\[
 p_{s_j}^{(\alpha,d)}(A_j)
 \le C_{\alpha,d}(s_j/R_j)^\beta
 =C_{\alpha,d}\kappa^\beta w_j.
\]
Choose \(\kappa\) so that
\(C_{\alpha,d}\kappa^\beta<c_{\alpha,d}\).  Data processing to
\(\{A_j,A_j^c\}\) and
Lemma~\ref{lem:bernoulli-kl-linear-separation} then yield
\(H_{\alpha,d}(s_j)\ge c w_j\), and hence
\[
 s_j^4H_{\alpha,d}(s_j)
 \ge cR_j^4w_j^{1+4/\beta}
 =ca_j^{q_{\alpha,d}}R_j^{4-rq_{\alpha,d}}.
\]
Here \(q_{\alpha,d}=1+4/\beta\) and
\(r<p=4/q_{\alpha,d}\), so the exponent of \(R_j\) is positive.
Recursive choice of the radii makes \(s_j\to\infty\) and the last display
unbounded while preserving all moment assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[All-order stable critical remainder]
\label{lem:all-order-stable-critical-remainder}
Let \(r\ge1\), put \(\beta=d+\alpha\), and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-critical-exponents}
 q_{r,\alpha,d}:=\min\left\{2,1+\frac{2r}{d+\alpha}\right\},
 \qquad
 p_{r,\alpha,d}:=\frac{2r}{q_{r,\alpha,d}}
 =\max\left\{r,\frac{2r(d+\alpha)}{d+\alpha+2r}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Let \(q=q_{r,\alpha,d}\), \(p=p_{r,\alpha,d}\), and
\(m=\lfloor p\rfloor\).  If \(T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(y,z)\) is the Taylor
polynomial of \(F_y^{(\alpha,d)}(z)\) at zero through degree \(m\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-critical-remainder}
 \|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(z)-T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(\cdot,z)\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}
 \le C_{\alpha,d,r}
 \begin{cases}
  |z|^{m+1},&|z|\le1,\\
  |z|^p,&|z|>1,
 \end{cases}
 \le C_{\alpha,d,r}|z|^p.
\end{equation}
Consequently, if a probability law \(\lambda\) on \(\RR^d\) has finite
\(p\)th absolute moment and
\[
 q_s^\lambda(y):=\int F_y^{(\alpha,d)}(x/s)\lambda(\dd x),
\]
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-integrated-remainder}
 q_s^\lambda
 =1+\sum_{j=1}^m s^{-j}B_j^\lambda+r_s^\lambda,
 \qquad
 \|r_s^\lambda\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}=o(s^{-p}),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:all-order-stable-moment-modes}
 B_j^\lambda(y):=
 \frac{(-1)^j}{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}
 \sum_{|\gamma|=j}\frac{m_\gamma(\lambda)}{\gamma!}
 \partial^\gamma p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y),
 \qquad
 m_\gamma(\lambda):=\int x^\gamma\lambda(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Every \(B_j^\lambda\) in this display is bounded.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof of \eqref{eq:stable-translation-lq-growth} applies verbatim for
every fixed \(1<q\le2\) and gives
\[
 \|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(z)\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}
 \le C(1+|z|)^{\beta(1-1/q)}.
\]
The local Taylor argument in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:stable-critical-translation-remainder} gives the first line
of \eqref{eq:all-order-stable-critical-remainder}.  For \(|z|>1\), the
Taylor modes are bounded by
Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-kernel-input-package}, and
hence
\[
 \|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(z)-T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(\cdot,z)\|_q
 \le C\{|z|^{\beta(1-1/q)}+|z|^m\}\le C|z|^p.
\]
Indeed, \(m\le p\), while
\(\beta(1-1/q)\le p\); in the non-Hilbert regime
\(\beta>2r\), both sides of the latter inequality equal
\(2r\beta/(\beta+2r)\).  Since \(m+1>p\), the final inequality in
\eqref{eq:all-order-stable-critical-remainder} also holds on the unit ball.

Taylor's formula gives \eqref{eq:all-order-stable-integrated-remainder}
before its norm assertion.  For fixed \(x\), the local estimate implies
\[
 s^p\|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(x/s)
       -T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(\cdot,x/s)\|_q\longrightarrow0,
\]
whereas \eqref{eq:all-order-stable-critical-remainder} bounds this expression
by \(C|x|^p\).  Minkowski's inequality and dominated convergence prove the
claimed \(L^q\) remainder.  Boundedness of the modes follows again from
Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-kernel-input-package}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two-background critical Bregman transfer]
\label{lem:two-background-critical-bregman-transfer}
Let \(1<q\le2\), \(p\ge r>0\), and \(pq=2r\).  On a probability space
\((E,\Omega)\), suppose \(g_s\ge c_s>0\),
\[
 \int g_s\dd\Omega=1,\qquad c_s\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad \|g_s-1\|_{L^1(\Omega)}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Let \(B\in L^\infty(\Omega)\), and suppose
\[
 v_s=s^{-r}B+o(s^{-r})\quad\hbox{in }L^\infty(\Omega),
 \qquad \|e_s\|_{L^q(\Omega)}=o(s^{-p}).
\]
If \(g_s+v_s+e_s\ge0\) and both \(g_s\Omega\) and
\((g_s+v_s+e_s)\Omega\) are probability measures, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-background-critical-bregman-transfer}
 D_{\rm KL}((g_s+v_s+e_s)\Omega\|g_s\Omega)
 =\frac{s^{-2r}}2\int B^2\dd\Omega+o(s^{-2r}).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

The hypotheses on the denominator in this lemma deliberately stop short of
\(L^\infty\)-convergence to one.  They require only a positive lower bound
approaching one, mass normalization, and \(L^1\)-convergence.  This weaker
interface is what permits a heavy-tailed translated mixture to serve as the
background.

\begin{proof}
For \(g>0\), write \(\Psi_g(z)=g\Phi(z/g)\).  Scaling
Lemma~\ref{lem:critical-lq-kl-perturbation} shows, whenever
\(|v/g|\le1/4\) and \(g+v+e\ge0\), that
\[
 0\le\Psi_g(v+e)-\Psi_g(v)
      -\log(1+v/g)e\le C_q g^{1-q}|e|^q.
\]
For large \(s\), the hypotheses give \(g_s\ge1/2\) and
\(\|v_s/g_s\|_\infty\le1/4\).  Therefore H\"older's inequality yields
\begin{align*}
 &\left|D_{\rm KL}((g_s+v_s+e_s)\Omega\|g_s\Omega)
       -\int\Psi_{g_s}(v_s)\dd\Omega\right|\\
 &\qquad\le
 C\|v_s\|_{L^{q/(q-1)}}\|e_s\|_{L^q}
 +C\|e_s\|_{L^q}^q
 =o(s^{-r-p})+o(s^{-pq})=o(s^{-2r}).
\end{align*}
Uniform Taylor expansion at zero gives
\[
 \int\Psi_{g_s}(v_s)\dd\Omega
 =\frac12\int\frac{v_s^2}{g_s}\dd\Omega+O(\|v_s\|_\infty^3).
\]
Finally,
\[
 \left|\int B^2(g_s^{-1}-1)\dd\Omega\right|
 \le2\|B\|_\infty^2\|g_s-1\|_1\longrightarrow0,
\]
and \eqref{eq:two-background-critical-bregman-transfer} follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite-difference moment blocks]
\label{lem:stable-finite-difference-blocks}
Let \(r\ge1\), let \(e\) be a unit vector, and define
\[
 \sigma_R:=2^{1-r}\sum_{k=0}^r(-1)^{r-k}\binom rk
                 \delta_{(k+1)Re}=A_R^+-A_R^-.
\]
Then \(A_R^+\) and \(A_R^-\) are probability measures and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-block-moment-identities}
 \int x^\gamma\dd\sigma_R(x)=0\quad(|\gamma|<r),
 \qquad
 \int(e\cdot x)^r\dd\sigma_R(x)=2^{1-r}r!R^r.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The positive and negative binomial coefficients each sum to \(2^{r-1}\),
and the order-\(r\) forward-difference identity gives
\eqref{eq:stable-block-moment-identities}.  In particular, when \(r=1\),
\(\sigma_R=\delta_{2Re}-\delta_{Re}\); the same cancellation and nonzero
first-difference conclusions hold without a separate construction.

The generalization does hold, but the correct arbitrary-order energy is not \(\int\Phi(V_s^\nu-V_s^\eta)\): the raw-tail difference is signed, so that expression can leave \(\Phi\)’s domain and loses nonnegativity. The repair is canonical. Retain the common Taylor jet through order \(r-1\) in both positive proxy densities, attach each law’s own unexpanded tail mixture, and take the relative \(\Phi\)-energy between those two proxies. Finite \(r\)-th moments make the omitted interior/tail Taylor terms \(o(s^{-r})\), and a new two-background expansion shows that the remaining contribution is exactly the universal order-\(r\) quadratic form up to \(o(s^{-2r})\).

I’ll also make compact abstract/introduction updates without adding another theorem environment; the proof will explicitly identify the inherited raw-tail argument and the new signed-tail/two-background step.
